<span class="atom-split" data-atom="richard_ii" data-title="RICHARD II" data-chapter="" data-kind="intro"></span>


[[Richard II|RICHARD II]]
THE subject matter of [[Richard II]] seemed inflammatorily topical to Shakespeare’s contemporaries. [[Richard]], who had notoriously indulged his favourites, had been compelled to yield his throne to [[Henry Bolingbroke]], Earl of Hereford: like [[Richard]], the ageing [[Queen Elizabeth]] had no obvious successor, and she too encouraged favourites—such as the Earl of Essex—who might aspire to the throne. When Shakespeare’s play first appeared in print (in 1597), and in the two succeeding editions printed during Elizabeth’s life, the episode (4.1.145-308) showing [[Richard]] yielding the [[Crown|crown]] was omitted, and in 1601, on the day before Essex led his ill-fated rebellion against Elizabeth, his fellow conspirators commissioned a special performance in the [[Hope|hope]] of arousing popular support, even though the play was said to be ‘long out of use’—surprisingly, since it was probably written no earlier than 1595.
But Shakespeare introduced no obvious topicality into his dramatization of [[Richard]]’s reign, for which he read widely while using Raphael Holinshed’s Chronicles (1577, revised and enlarged in 1587) as his main source of information. In choosing to write about [[Richard II]] (1367―1400) he was returning to the beginning of the story whose ending he had staged in [[Richard III]]; for Bolingbroke’s usurpation of the throne to which [[Richard]]’s hereditary right was indisputable had set in train the series of events finally expiated only in the [[Union|union]] of the houses of York and Lancaster celebrated in the last speech of [[Richard III]]. Like [[Richard III]], this is a tragical [[history]], focusing on a single character; but [[Richard II]] is a far more introverted and morally ambiguous figure than [[Richard III]]. In this play, written entirely in verse, Shakespeare forgoes stylistic variety in favour of an intense, plangent lyricism.
Our early impressions of [[Richard]] are unsympathetic. Having banished [[Mowbray]] and Bolingbroke, he behaves callously to Bolingbroke’s father, [[John of Gaunt]], a stern upholder of the old order to whose warning against his irresponsible behaviour he pays no attention, and upon Gaunt’s [[Death|death]] confiscates his property with no regard for Bolingbroke’s rights. During [[Richard]]’s absence on an Irish campaign, Bolingbroke returns to England and gains support in his efforts to claim his inheritance. Gradually, as the balance of power shifts, [[Richard]] makes deeper claims on the audience’s sympathy. When he confronts Bolingbroke at Flint [[Castle]] (3.1) he eloquently laments his imminent deposition even though Bolingbroke insists that he comes only to claim what is his; soon afterwards (4.1.98-103) the [[Duke of York]] announces [[Richard]]’s abdication. The transference of power is effected in a scene of lyrical expansiveness, and [[Richard]] becomes a pitiable figure as he is led to [[Imprisonment|imprisonment]] in Pomfret (Pontefract) [[Castle]] while his former [[Queen|queen]] is banished to France. [[Richard]]’s [[Self|self]]-exploration reaches its climax in his soliloquy spoken shortly before his murder at the hands of Piers Exton; at the end of the play, Henry, anxious and [[Guilt|guilt]]-laden, denies responsibility for the murder and plans an expiatory pilgrimage to the Holy Land.

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] [[Richard]] and [[John of Gaunt]], with the Lord Marshal, other nobles, and attendants |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Old [[John of Gaunt]], [[Time\|time]]-honoured Lancaster,<br>Hast thou according to thy oath and bond<br>Brought hither Henry Hereford, thy bold son,<br>Here to make good the boist’rous late appeal,<br>Which then our leisure would not let us hear,<br>Against the Duke of Norfolk, Thomas [[Mowbray]]? |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | I have, my liege. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Tell me moreover, hast thou sounded him<br>If he appeal the Duke on ancient malice<br>Or worthily, as a good subject should,<br>On some known ground of treachery in him? |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | As near as I could sift him on that argument,<br>On some apparent danger seen in him<br>Aimed at your highness, no inveterate malice. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Then call them to our presence.<br>[Exit one or more]<br>Face to face<br>And frowning brow to brow, ourselves will hear<br>The accuser and the accused freely speak.<br>High-stomached are they both and full of ire;<br>In rage, deaf as the sea, hasty as [[Fire\|fire]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bolingbroke Duke of Hereford, and [[Mowbray]] Duke of Norfolk |
| BOLINGBROKE | Many years of happy days befall<br>My gracious sovereign, my most loving liege! |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Each day still better others’ happiness,<br>Until the heavens, envying earth’s good hap,<br>Add an immortal title to your [[Crown\|crown]]! |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | We thank you both. Yet one but flatters us,<br>As [[Well\|well]] appeareth by the cause you come,<br>Namely, to appeal each other of high treason.<br>Cousin of Hereford, what dost thou object<br>Against the Duke of Norfolk, Thomas [[Mowbray]]? |
| BOLINGBROKE | First—heaven be the record to my speech—<br>In the devotion of a subject’s [[Love\|love]],<br>Tend’ring the precious safety of my Prince,<br>And free from other misbegotten hate,<br>Come I appellant to this princely presence.<br>Now, Thomas [[Mowbray]], do I turn to thee;<br>And mark my greeting [[Well\|well]], for what I speak<br>My [[Body\|body]] shall make good upon this earth,<br>Or my divine [[Soul\|soul]] answer it in heaven.<br>Thou art a traitor and a miscreant,<br>Too good to be so, and too bad to live,<br>Since the more fair and crystal is the sky,<br>The uglier seem the clouds that in it fly.<br>Once more, the more to aggravate the note,<br>With a foul traitor’s name stuff I thy throat,<br>And wish, so please my sovereign, ere I move<br>What my tongue speaks my right-drawn [[Sword\|sword]] may<br>prove. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Let not my cold words here accuse my zeal.<br>’Tis not the trial of a woman’s war,<br>The bitter clamour of two eager tongues,<br>Can arbitrate this cause betwixt us twain.<br>The [[Blood\|blood]] is hot that must be cooled for this.<br>Yet can I not of such tame patience boast<br>As to be hushed and naught at all to say.<br>First, the fair reverence of your highness curbs me<br>From giving reins and spurs to my free speech,<br>Which else would post until it had returned<br>These terms of treason doubled down his throat.<br>Setting aside his high [[Blood\|blood]]’s royalty,<br>And let him be no kinsman to my liege,<br>I do defy him, and I spit at him,<br>Call him a slanderous coward and a [[Villain\|villain]];<br>Which to maintain I would allow him odds,<br>And meet him, were I tied to run afoot<br>Even to the frozen ridges of the Alps,<br>Or any other ground inhabitable,<br>Wherever Englishman durst set his foot.<br>Meantime let this defend my loyalty:<br>By all my hopes, most falsely doth he lie. |
| BOLINGBROKE | (throwing down his gage)<br>Pale trembling coward, there I throw my gage,<br>Disclaiming here the kindred of the [[King]],<br>And lay aside my high [[Blood\|blood]]’s royalty,<br>Which [[Fear\|fear]], not reverence, makes thee to except.<br>If guilty dread have left thee so much strength<br>As to take up mine [[Honour\|honour]]’s pawn, then stoop.<br>By that, and all the rites of knighthood else,<br>Will I make good against thee, arm to arm,<br>What I have spoke or thou canst worse devise. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | (taking up the gage)<br>I take it up, and by that [[Sword\|sword]] I swear<br>Which gently laid my knighthood on my shoulder,<br>I’ll answer thee in any fair degree<br>Or chivalrous design of knightly trial;<br>And when I mount, alive may I not light<br>If I be traitor or unjustly fight! |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | (to Bolingbroke)<br>What doth our cousin lay to [[Mowbray]]’s charge?<br>It must be great that can inherit us<br>So much as of a thought of ill in him. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Look what I speak, my life shall prove it true:<br>That [[Mowbray]] hath received eight thousand nobles<br>In name of lendings for your highness’ soldiers,<br>The which he hath detained for lewd employments,<br>Like a false traitor and injurious [[Villain\|villain]].<br>Besides I say, and will in battle prove,<br>Or here or elsewhere, to the furthest verge<br>That ever was surveyed by English eye,<br>That all the treasons for these eighteen years<br>Complotted and contrived in this land<br>Fetch from false [[Mowbray]] their first head and [[Spring\|spring]].<br>Further I say, and further will maintain<br>Upon his bad life, to make all this good,<br>That he did plot the [[Duke of Gloucester]]’s [[Death\|death]],<br>Suggest his soon-believing adversaries,<br>And consequently, like a traitor-coward,<br>Sluiced out his [[Innocent\|innocent]] [[Soul\|soul]] through streams of [[Blood\|blood]];<br>Which [[Blood\|blood]], like sacrificing Abel’s, cries<br>Even from the tongueless caverns of the earth<br>To me for justice and rough chastisement.<br>And, by the glorious worth of my descent,<br>This arm shall do it or this life be spent. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | How high a pitch his resolution soars!<br>Thomas of Norfolk, what sayst thou to this? |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | O, let my sovereign turn away his face,<br>And bid his ears a little while be deaf,<br>Till I have told this slander of his [[Blood\|blood]]<br>How God and good men hate so foul a liar! |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | [[Mowbray]], impartial are our eyes and ears.<br>Were he my brother, nay, my kingdom’s heir,<br>As he is but my father’s brother’s son,<br>Now by my sceptre’s awe I make a vow<br>Such neighbour-nearness to our sacred [[Blood\|blood]]<br>Should nothing privilege him, nor partialize<br>The unstooping firmness of my upright [[Soul\|soul]].<br>He is our subject, [[Mowbray]]; so art thou.<br>Free speech and fearless I to thee allow. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Then, Bolingbroke, as low as to thy heart<br>Through the false passage of thy throat thou liest!<br>Three parts of that receipt I had for Calais<br>Disbursed I duly to his highness’ soldiers.<br>The other part reserved I by consent,<br>For that my sovereign liege was in my debt<br>Upon remainder of a dear account<br>Since last I went to France to fetch his [[Queen\|queen]].<br>Now [[Swallow\|swallow]] down that lie. For [[Gloucester]]’s [[Death\|death]],<br>I slew him not, but to my own disgrace<br>Neglected my sworn duty in that case.<br>For you, my noble lord of Lancaster,<br>The honourable father to my foe,<br>Once did I lay an ambush for your life,<br>A trespass that doth vex my grieved [[Soul\|soul]];<br>But ere I last received the Sacrament<br>I did confess it, and exactly begged<br>Your grace’s pardon, and I [[Hope\|hope]] I had it.<br>This is my fault. As for the rest appealed,<br>It issues from the rancour of a [[Villain\|villain]],<br>A recreant and most degenerate traitor,<br>Which in myself I boldly will defend,<br>He throws down his gage<br>And interchangeably hurl down my gage<br>Upon this overweening traitor’s foot,<br>To prove myself a loyal gentleman<br>Even in the best [[Blood\|blood]] chambered in his bosom;<br>In haste whereof most heartily I pray<br>Your highness to assign our trial day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Bolingbroke takes up the gage |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Wrath-kindled gentlemen, be ruled by me.<br>Let’s purge this choler without letting [[Blood\|blood]].<br>This we prescribe, though no physician:<br>Deep malice makes too deep incision;<br>Forget, forgive, conclude, and be agreed;<br>Our doctors say this is no [[Time\|time]] to bleed.<br>Good uncle, let this end where it begun.<br>We’ll calm the Duke of Norfolk, you your son. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | To be a make-peace shall become my age.<br>Throw down, my son, the Duke of Norfolk’s gage. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | And, Norfolk, throw down his. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | When, Harry, when?<br>Obedience bids I should not bid again. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Norfolk, throw down! We bid; there is no boot. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | (kneeling)<br>Myself I throw, dread sovereign, at thy foot.<br>My life thou shalt command, but not my shame.<br>The one my duty owes, but my fair name,<br>Despite of [[Death\|death]] that lives upon my [[Grave\|grave]],<br>To dark dishonour’s use thou shalt not have.<br>I am disgraced, impeached, and baffled here,<br>Pierced to the [[Soul\|soul]] with slander’s venomed spear,<br>The which no balm can cure but his heart [[Blood\|blood]]<br>Which breathed this [[Poison\|poison]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Rage must be withstood.<br>Give me his gage. Lions make leopards tame. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] standing |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Yea, but not change his spots. Take but my shame,<br>And I resign my gage. My dear dear lord,<br>The purest treasure mortal times afford<br>Is spotless reputation; that away,<br>Men are but gilded loam, or painted clay.<br>A [[Jewel\|jewel]] in a ten-times barred-up chest<br>Is a bold spirit in a loyal breast.<br>Mine [[Honour\|honour]] is my life. Both grow in one.<br>Take [[Honour\|honour]] from me, and my life is done.<br>Then, dear my liege, mine [[Honour\|honour]] let me try.<br>In that I live, and for that will I die. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Cousin, throw down your gage. Do you begin. |
| BOLINGBROKE | O God defend my [[Soul\|soul]] from such deep sin!<br>Shall I seem crest-fallen in my father’s sight?<br>Or with pale beggar-[[Fear\|fear]] impeach my height<br>Before this out-dared dastard? Ere my tongue<br>Shall wound my [[Honour\|honour]] with such feeble wrong,<br>Or sound so base a parle, my teeth shall tear<br>The slavish motive of recanting [[Fear\|fear]],<br>And spit it bleeding in his high disgrace<br>Where shame doth harbour, even in [[Mowbray]]’s face. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[John of Gaunt]] |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | We were not born to sue, but to command;<br>Which since we cannot do to make you friends,<br>Be ready, as your lives shall answer it,<br>At Coventry upon Saint Lambert’s day.<br>There shall your swords and lances arbitrate<br>The swelling difference of your settled hate.<br>Since we cannot atone you, we shall see<br>Justice design the victor’s chivalry.<br>Lord Marshal, command our officers-at-arms<br>Be ready to direct these [[Home\|home]] alarms. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[John of Gaunt]], Duke of Lancaster, with the Duchess of [[Gloucester]] |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Alas, the part I had in [[Gloucester]]’s [[Blood\|blood]]<br>Doth more solicit me than your exclaims<br>To stir against the butchers of his life.<br>But since correction lieth in those hands<br>Which made the fault that we cannot correct,<br>Put we our quarrel to the will of heaven,<br>Who, when they see the hours ripe on earth,<br>Will rain hot vengeance on offenders’ heads. |
| DUCHESS OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Finds brotherhood in thee no sharper spur?<br>Hath [[Love\|love]] in thy old [[Blood\|blood]] no living [[Fire\|fire]]?<br>Edward’s seven sons, whereof thyself art one,<br>Were as seven vials of his sacred [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Or seven fair branches springing from one root.<br>Some of those seven are dried by [[Nature\|nature]]’s course,<br>Some of those branches by the destinies cut;<br>But Thomas, my dear lord, my life, my [[Gloucester]],<br>One vial full of Edward’s sacred [[Blood\|blood]],<br>One flourishing branch of his most royal root,<br>Is cracked, and all the precious liquor spilt;<br>Is hacked down, and his [[Summer\|summer]] [[Leaves\|leaves]] all faded<br>By envy’s hand and murder’s bloody axe.<br>Ah, Gaunt, his [[Blood\|blood]] was thine! That bed, that womb,<br>That mettle, that [[Self\|self]] mould that fashioned thee,<br>Made him a man; and though thou liv‘st and<br>breathest,<br>Yet art thou slain in him. Thou dost consent<br>In some large measure to thy father’s [[Death\|death]]<br>In that thou seest thy wretched brother die,<br>Who was the model of thy father’s life.<br>Call it not patience, Gaunt, it is [[Despair\|despair]].<br>In suff’ring thus thy brother to be slaughtered<br>Thou show’st the naked pathway to thy life,<br>Teaching stern murder how to butcher thee.<br>That which in mean men we entitle patience<br>Is pale cold cowardice in noble breasts.<br>What shall I say? To safeguard thine own life<br>The best way is to venge my [[Gloucester]]’s [[Death\|death]]. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | God’s is the quarrel; for God’s substitute,<br>His deputy anointed in his sight,<br>Hath caused his [[Death\|death]]; the which if wrongfully,<br>Let heaven [[Revenge\|revenge]], for I may never lift<br>An angry arm against his minister. |
| DUCHESS OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Where then, alas, may I complain myself? |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | To God, the widow’s champion and defence. |
| DUCHESS OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Why then, I will. Farewell, old Gaunt.<br>Thou goest to Coventry, there to behold<br>Our cousin Hereford and fell [[Mowbray]] fight.<br>O, set my husband’s wrongs on Hereford’s spear,<br>That it may enter butcher [[Mowbray]]’s breast!<br>Or if misfortune miss the first career,<br>Be [[Mowbray]]’s sins so heavy in his bosom<br>That they may break his foaming courser’s back<br>And throw the rider headlong in the lists,<br>A caitiff, recreant to my cousin Hereford!<br>Farewell, old Gaunt. Thy sometimes brother’s wife<br>With her companion, grief, must end her life. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Sister, farewell. I must to Coventry.<br>As much good stay with thee as go with me. |
| DUCHESS OF [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Yet one word more. Grief boundeth where it falls,<br>Not with the empty hollowness, but weight.<br>I take my leave before I have begun,<br>For sorrow ends not when it seemeth done.<br>Commend me to thy brother, [[Edmund]] York.<br>Lo, this is all.—Nay, yet depart not so!<br>Though this be all, do not so quickly go.<br>I shall remember more. Bid him—ah, what?—<br>With all good [[Speed\|speed]] at Pleshey visit me.<br>Alack, and what shall good old York there see<br>But empty lodgings and unfurnished walls,<br>Unpeopled offices, untrodden stones,<br>And what hear there for welcome but my groans?<br>Therefore commend me; let him not come there<br>To seek out sorrow that dwells everywhere.<br>Desolate, desolate will I hence and die.<br>The last leave of thee takes my weeping eye. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 1, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lord Marshal [with officers setting out chairs], and the [[Duke of Aumerle]] |
| LORD MARSHAL | My lord Aumerle, is Harry Hereford armed? |
| AUMERLE | Yea, at all points, and longs to enter in. |
| LORD MARSHAL | The Duke of Norfolk, sprightfully and bold,<br>Stays but the summons of the appellant’s trumpet. |
| AUMERLE | Why then, the champions are prepared, and stay<br>For nothing but his majesty’s approach. |
| *(didascalia)* | The trumpets sound, and [[King]] [[Richard]] enters, with [[John of Gaunt]], Duke of Lancaster, Bushy, Bagot, Green,] and other nobles. When they are set, enter [[Mowbray]] Duke of Norfolk, defendant, in arms, Fand a Herald] |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Marshal, demand of yonder champion<br>The cause of his arrival here in arms.<br>Ask him his name, and orderly proceed<br>To swear him in the justice of his cause. |
| LORD MARSHAL | (to [[Mowbray]])<br>In God’s name and the [[King]]‘s, say who thou art,<br>And why thou com’st thus knightly clad in arms,<br>Against what man thou com’st, and what thy<br>quarrel.<br>Speak truly on thy knighthood and thy oath,<br>As so defend thee heaven and thy valour! |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | My name is Thomas [[Mowbray]], Duke of Norfolk,<br>Who hither come engaged by my oath—<br>Which God defend a knight should violate—<br>Both to defend my loyalty and [[Truth\|truth]]<br>To God, my [[King\|king]], and my succeeding issue,<br>Against the Duke of Hereford that appeals me;<br>And by the grace of God and this mine arm<br>To prove him, in defending of myself,<br>A traitor to my God, my [[King\|king]], and me.<br>And as I truly fight, defend me heaven! |
| *(didascalia)* | He sits. |
| *(didascalia)* | The trumpets sound. Enter Bolingbroke Duke of Hereford, appellant, in armour, and a Herald |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Marshal, ask yonder knight in arms<br>Both who he is and why he cometh hither<br>Thus plated in habiliments of war;<br>And formally, according to our law,<br>Depose him in the justice of his cause. |
| LORD MARSHAL | (to Bolingbroke)<br>What is thy name? And wherefore com’st thou hither<br>Before [[King]] [[Richard]] in his royal lists?<br>Against whom comest thou? And what’s thy quarrel?<br>Speak like a true knight, so defend thee heaven! |
| BOLINGBROKE | Harry of Hereford, Lancaster, and Derby<br>Am I, who ready here do stand in arms<br>To prove by God’s grace and my [[Body\|body]]’s valour<br>In lists on Thomas [[Mowbray]], Duke of Norfolk,<br>That he is a traitor foul and dangerous<br>To God of heaven, [[King]] [[Richard]], and to me.<br>And as I truly fight, defend me heaven! |
| *(didascalia)* | He sits |
| LORD MARSHAL | On [[Pain\|pain]] of [[Death\|death]], no person be so bold<br>Or daring-hardy as to touch the lists<br>Except the Marshal and such officers<br>Appointed to direct these fair designs. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOLINGBROKE standing |
| LORD MARSHAL | Lord Marshal, let me kiss my sovereign’s hand<br>And bow my knee before his majesty,<br>For [[Mowbray]] and myself are like two men<br>That vow a long and weary pilgrimage;<br>Then let us take a ceremonious leave<br>And loving farewell of our several friends. |
| LORD MARSHAL | (to [[King]] [[Richard]])<br>The appellant in all duty greets your highness,<br>And craves to kiss your hand and take his leave. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | We will descend and fold him in our arms.<br>He descends from his seat and embraces Bolingbroke<br>Cousin of Hereford, as thy cause is just,<br>So be thy fortune in this royal fight.<br>Farewell, my [[Blood\|blood]], which if today thou shed,<br>Lament we may, but not [[Revenge\|revenge]] thee dead. |
| BOLINGBROKE | O, let no noble eye profane a tear<br>For me if I be gored with [[Mowbray]]’s spear.<br>As confident as is the falcon’s flight<br>Against a bird do I with [[Mowbray]] fight.<br>(To the Lord Marshal) My loving lord, I take my leave of you;<br>(To Aumerle) Of you, my noble cousin, Lord Aumerle;<br>Not sick, although I have to do with [[Death\|death]],<br>But lusty, young, and cheerly drawing breath.<br>Lo, as at English feasts, so I regreet<br>The daintiest last, to make the end most sweet. |
| *(didascalia)* | (To Gaunt, kneeling O thou, the earthly author of my [[Blood\|blood]], |
| BOLINGBROKE | Whose youthful spirit in me regenerate<br>Doth with a two-fold vigour lift me up<br>To reach at victory above my head,<br>Add proof unto mine armour with thy prayers,<br>And with thy blessings steel my lance’s point,<br>That it may enter [[Mowbray]]’s waxen coat<br>And furbish new the name of John a Gaunt<br>Even in the lusty haviour of his son. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | God in thy good cause make thee prosperous!<br>Be swift like [[Lightning\|lightning]] in the execution,<br>And let thy blows, doubly redoubled,<br>Fall like amazing thunder on the casque<br>Of thy adverse pernicious enemy.<br>Rouse up thy youthful [[Blood\|blood]], be valiant, and live. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOLINGBROKE standing |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Mine [[innocence]] and Saint George to thrive! |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] standing |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | However God or fortune cast my lot,<br>There lives or dies, true to [[King]] [[Richard]]’s throne,<br>A loyal, just, and upright gentleman.<br>Never did captive with a freer heart<br>Cast off his [[Chains\|chains]] of bondage and embrace<br>His golden uncontrolled enfranchisement<br>More than my dancing [[Soul\|soul]] doth celebrate<br>This feast of battle with mine adversary.<br>Most mighty liege, and my companion peers,<br>Take from my mouth the wish of happy years.<br>As gentle and as jocund as to jest<br>Go I to fight. [[Truth]] hath a quiet breast. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Farewell, my lord. Securely I espy<br>Virtue with valour couched in thine eye.—<br>Order the trial, Marshal, and begin. |
| LORD MARSHAL | Harry of Hereford, Lancaster, and Derby,<br>Receive thy lance; and God defend the right! |
| *(didascalia)* | An officer bears a lance to Bolingbroke |
| BOLINGBROKE | Strong as a tower in [[Hope\|hope]], I cry ‘Amen!’ |
| LORD MARSHAL | (to an officer)<br>Go bear this lance to Thomas, Duke of Norfolk. |
| *(didascalia)* | An officer bears a lance to [[Mowbray]] |
| FIRST HERALD | Harry of Hereford, Lancaster, and Derby<br>Stands here for God, his sovereign, and himself,<br>On [[Pain\|pain]] to be found false and recreant,<br>To prove the Duke of Norfolk, Thomas [[Mowbray]],<br>A traitor to his God, his [[King\|king]], and him,<br>And dares him to set forward to the fight. |
| SECOND HERALD | Here standeth Thomas [[Mowbray]], Duke of Norfolk,<br>On [[Pain\|pain]] to be found false and recreant,<br>Both to defend himself and to approve<br>Henry of Hereford, Lancaster, and Derby<br>To God his sovereign and to him disloyal,<br>Courageously and with a free [[Desire\|desire]]<br>Attending but the signal to begin. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound trumpets, and set forward combatants! |
| *(didascalia)* | A charge is sounded. |
| LORD MARSHAL | [[King]] [[Richard]] throws down his warder<br>Stay, the [[King]] hath thrown his warder down. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Let them lay by their helmets and their spears,<br>And both return back to their chairs again. |
| *(didascalia)* | Bolingbroke and [[Mowbray]] disarm and sit |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | (To the nobles) Withdraw with us, and let the trumpets sound<br>While we return these dukes what we decree. |
| *(didascalia)* | A long flourish, during which [[King]] [[Richard]] and his nobles withdraw and hold council, then come forward]. [[King]] [[Richard]] addresses Bolingbroke and [[Mowbray]] |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Draw near, and list what with our council we have<br>done.<br>For that our kingdom’s earth should not be soiled<br>With that dear [[Blood\|blood]] which it hath fostered,<br>And for our eyes do hate the dire aspect<br>Of civil wounds ploughed up with neighbours’ swords,<br>Which, so roused up with boist’rous untuned drums,<br>With harsh-resounding trumpets’ dreadful bray,<br>And grating shock of wrathful iron arms,<br>Might from our quiet confines fright fair peace<br>And make us wade even in our kindred’s [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Therefore we banish you our territories.<br>You, cousin Hereford, upon [[Pain\|pain]] of life,<br>Till twice five summers have enriched our fields<br>Shall not regreet our fair dominions,<br>But tread the stranger paths of banishment. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Your will be done. This must my comfort be:<br>That sun that warms you here shall shine on me,<br>And those his golden beams to you here lent<br>Shall point on me and gild my banishment. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Norfolk, for thee remains a heavier doom,<br>Which I with some unwillingness pronounce.<br>The sly slow hours shall not determinate<br>The dateless limit of thy dear [[Exile\|exile]].<br>The hopeless word of ‘never to return’<br>Breathe I against thee, upon [[Pain\|pain]] of life. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | A heavy sentence, my most sovereign liege,<br>And all unlooked-for from your highness’ mouth.<br>A dearer merit, not so deep a maim<br>As to be cast forth in the common air,<br>Have I deserved at your highness’ hands.<br>The language I have learnt these forty years,<br>My native English, now I must forgo,<br>And now my tongue’s use is to me no more<br>Than an unstringèd viol or a harp,<br>Or like a cunning instrument cased up,<br>Or, being open, put into his hands<br>That knows no touch to tune the harmony. |
| *(didascalia)* | Within my mouth you have enjailed my tongue, |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Doubly portcullised with my teeth and lips,<br>And dull unfeeling barren ignorance<br>Is made my jailer to attend on me.<br>I am too old to fawn upon a [[Nurse\|nurse]],<br>Too far in years to be a pupil now.<br>What is thy sentence then but speechless [[Death\|death]],<br>Which robs my tongue from breathing native breath? |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | It boots thee not to be compassionate.<br>After our sentence, plaining comes too late. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Then thus I turn me from my country’s light,<br>To dwell in solemn shades of endless [[Night\|night]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Return again, and take an oath with thee.<br>(To both) Lay on our royal [[Sword\|sword]] your banished hands.<br>Swear by the duty that you owe to God—<br>Our part therein we banish with yourselves—<br>To keep the oath that we administer.<br>You never shall, so help you [[Truth\|truth]] and God,<br>Embrace each other’s [[Love\|love]] in banishment,<br>Nor never look upon each other’s face,<br>Nor never write, regreet, nor reconcile<br>This low‘ring tempest of your [[Home\|home]]-bred hate,<br>Nor never by advised purpose meet<br>To plot, contrive, or complot any ill<br>’Gainst us, our state, our subjects, or our land. |
| BOLINGBROKE | I swear. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | And I, to keep all this. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Norfolk, so far as to mine enemy:<br>By this [[Time\|time]], had the [[King]] permitted us,<br>One of our souls had wandered in the air,<br>Banished this frail sepulchre of our flesh,<br>As now our flesh is banished from this land.<br>Confess thy treasons ere thou fly the realm.<br>Since thou hast far to go, bear not along<br>The clogging burden of a guilty [[Soul\|soul]]. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | No, Bolingbroke, if ever I were traitor,<br>My name be blotted from the book of life,<br>And I from heaven banished as from hence.<br>But what thou art, God, thou, and I do know,<br>And all too soon I [[Fear\|fear]] the [[King]] shall rue.<br>Farewell, my liege. Now no way can I stray:<br>Save back to England, all the world’s my way. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Uncle, even in the glasses of thine eyes<br>I see thy grieved heart. Thy sad aspect<br>Hath from the number of his banished years<br>Plucked four away. (To Bolingbroke) Six frozen winters<br>spent,<br>Return with welcome [[Home\|home]] from banishment. |
| BOLINGBROKE | How long a [[Time\|time]] lies in one little word!<br>Four lagging winters and four wanton springs<br>End in a word: such is the breath of kings. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | I thank my liege that in regard of me<br>He shortens four years of my son’s [[Exile\|exile]].<br>But little vantage shall I reap thereby,<br>For ere the six years that he hath to spend<br>Can change their moons and bring their times about,<br>My oil-dried lamp and [[Time\|time]]-bewasted light<br>Shall be extinct with age and endless [[Night\|night]].<br>My inch of taper will be burnt and done,<br>And blindfold [[Death\|death]] not let me see my son. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Why, uncle, thou hast many years to live. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | But not a minute, [[King]], that thou canst give.<br>Shorten my days thou canst with sudden sorrow,<br>And pluck nights from me, but not lend a morrow.<br>Thou canst help [[Time\|time]] to furrow me with age,<br>But stop no wrinkle in his pilgrimage.<br>Thy word is current with him for my [[Death\|death]],<br>But dead, thy kingdom cannot buy my breath. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Thy son is banished upon good advice,<br>Whereto thy tongue a party verdict gave.<br>Why at our justice seem’st thou then to lour? |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Things sweet to taste prove in digestion sour.<br>You urged me as a judge, but I had rather<br>You would have bid me argue like a father.<br>Alas, I looked when some of you should say<br>I was too strict to make mine own away,<br>But you gave leave to my unwilling tongue<br>Against my will to do myself this wrong. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Cousin, farewell; and uncle, bid him so.<br>Six years we banish him, and he shall go. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt all but Aumerle, the Lord Marshal, [[John of Gaunt]], and Bolingbroke |
| AUMERLE | (to Bolingbroke)<br>Cousin, farewell. What presence must not know,<br>From where you do remain let paper show.<br>[Exit] |
| LORD MARSHAL | (to Bolingbroke)<br>My lord, no leave take I, for I will ride<br>As far as land will let me by your side. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | (to Bolingbroke)<br>O, to what purpose dost thou hoard thy words,<br>That thou return’st no greeting to thy friends? |
| BOLINGBROKE | I have too few to take my leave of you,<br>When the tongue’s office should be prodigal<br>To breathe the abundant dolour of the heart. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Thy grief is but thy absence for a [[Time\|time]]. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Joy absent, grief is present for that [[Time\|time]]. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | What is six winters? They are quickly gone. |
| BOLINGBROKE | To men in joy, but grief makes one hour ten. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Call it a travel that thou tak’st for pleasure. |
| BOLINGBROKE | My heart will sigh when I miscall it so,<br>Which finds it an enforced pilgrimage. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | The sullen passage of thy weary steps<br>Esteem as foil wherein thou art to set<br>The precious [[Jewel\|jewel]] of thy [[Home\|home]] return. |
| BOLINGBROKE | O, who can hold a [[Fire\|fire]] in his hand<br>By thinking on the frosty Caucasus,<br>Or cloy the hungry edge of appetite<br>By bare [[Imagination\|imagination]] of a feast,<br>Or wallow naked in December [[Snow\|snow]]<br>By thinking on fantastic [[Summer\|summer]]’s heat?<br>O no, the apprehension of the good<br>Gives but the greater feeling to the worse.<br>Fell sorrow’s tooth doth never rankle more<br>Than when he bites, but lanceth not the sore. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Come, come, my son, I’ll bring thee on thy way.<br>Had I thy youth and cause, I would not stay. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Then England’s ground, farewell. Sweet soil, adieu,<br>My mother and my [[Nurse\|nurse]] that bears me yet!<br>Where’er I wander, boast of this I can:<br>Though banished, yet a trueborn Englishman. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] [[Richard]] with Green and Bagot at one door, and the Lord Aumerle at another |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | We did observe.—Cousin Aumerle,<br>How far brought you high Hereford on his way? |
| AUMERLE | I brought high Hereford, if you call him so,<br>But to the next highway, and there I left him. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | And say, what store of parting [[Tears\|tears]] were shed? |
| AUMERLE | [[Faith]], none for me, except the north-east [[Wind\|wind]],<br>Which then grew bitterly against our faces,<br>Awaked the sleeping rheum, and so by chance<br>Did grace our hollow parting with a tear. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | What said our cousin when you parted with him? |
| AUMERLE | ‘Farewell.’ And for my heart disdained that my tongue<br>Should so profane the word, that taught me craft<br>To counterfeit oppression of such grief<br>That words seemed buried in my sorrow’s [[Grave\|grave]].<br>Marry, would the word ‘farewell’ have lengthened<br>hours<br>And added years to his short banishment,<br>He should have had a volume of farewells;<br>But since it would not, he had none of me. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | He is our cousin, cousin; but ‘tis [[Doubt\|doubt]],<br>When [[Time\|time]] shall call him [[Home\|home]] from banishment,<br>Whether our kinsman come to see his friends.<br>Ourself and Bushy, Bagot here, and Green<br>Observed his courtship to the common people,<br>How he did seem to dive into their hearts<br>With humble and familiar courtesy,<br>What reverence he did throw away on slaves,<br>Wooing poor craftsmen with the craft of smiles<br>And patient underbearing of his fortune,<br>As ’twere to banish their affects with him.<br>Off goes his bonnet to an oysterwench.<br>A brace of draymen bid God [[Speed\|speed]] him [[Well\|well]],<br>And had the tribute of his supple knee<br>With ‘Thanks, my countrymen, my loving friends’,<br>As were our England in reversion his,<br>And he our subjects’ next degree in [[Hope\|hope]]. |
| GREEN | [[Well]], he is gone, and with him go these thoughts.<br>Now for the rebels which stand out in Ireland.<br>Expedient manage must be made, my liege,<br>Ere further leisure yield them further means<br>For their advantage and your highness’ loss. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | We will ourself in person to this war,<br>And for our coffers with too great a [[Court\|court]]<br>And liberal largess are grown somewhat light,<br>We are enforced to farm our royal realm,<br>The revenue whereof shall furnish us<br>For our affairs in hand. If that come short,<br>Our substitutes at [[Home\|home]] shall have blank charters,<br>Whereto, when they shall know what men are rich,<br>They shall subscribe them for large sums of gold,<br>And send them after to supply our wants;<br>For we will make for Ireland presently. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bushy |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Bushy, what news? |
| BUSHY | Old [[John of Gaunt]] is grievous sick, my lord,<br>Suddenly taken, and hath sent post-haste<br>To entreat your majesty to visit him. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Where lies he? |
| BUSHY | At Ely [[house\|House]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Now put it, God, in his physician’s mind<br>To help him to his [[Grave\|grave]] immediately.<br>The lining of his coffers shall make coats<br>To deck our soldiers for these Irish wars.<br>Come, gentlemen, let’s all go visit him.<br>Pray God we may make haste and come too late! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[John of Gaunt]], Duke of Lancaster, sick, carried in a chair, with the [[Duke of York]] |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Will the [[King]] come, that I may breathe my last<br>In wholesome counsel to his unstaid youth? |
| YORK | Vex not yourself, nor strive not with your breath,<br>For all in vain comes counsel to his ear. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | O, but they say the tongues of dying men<br>Enforce attention, like deep harmony.<br>Where words are scarce they are seldom spent in vain,<br>For they breathe [[Truth\|truth]] that breathe their words in [[Pain\|pain]].<br>He that no more must say is listened more<br>Than they whom youth and ease have taught to glose.<br>More are men’s ends marked than their lives before.<br>The setting sun, and music at the close,<br>As the last taste of sweets, is sweetest last,<br>Writ in remembrance more than things long past.<br>Though [[Richard]] my life’s counsel would not hear,<br>My [[Death\|death]]’s sad tale may yet undeaf his ear. |
| YORK | No, it is stopped with other, flattering sounds,<br>As praises of whose taste the wise are feared,<br>Lascivious metres to whose venom sound<br>The open ear of youth doth always listen,<br>Report of fashions in proud [[Italy]],<br>Whose manners still our tardy-apish nation<br>Limps after in base [[Imitation\|imitation]].<br>Where doth the world thrust forth a [[Vanity\|vanity]]—<br>So it be new there’s no respect how vile—<br>That is not quickly buzzed into his ears?<br>Then all too late comes counsel, to be heard<br>Where will doth mutiny with wit’s regard.<br>Direct not him whose way himself will choose:<br>’Tis breath thou lack’st, and that breath wilt thou lose. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Methinks I am a [[Prophet\|prophet]] new-inspired,<br>And thus, expiring, do foretell of him.<br>His rash, fierce blaze of riot cannot last,<br>For violent fires soon burn out themselves.<br>Small showers last long, but sudden storms are short.<br>He tires betimes that spurs too fast betimes.<br>With eager feeding food doth choke the feeder.<br>Light [[Vanity\|vanity]], insatiate cormorant,<br>Consuming means, soon preys upon itself.<br>This royal throne of kings, this sceptred isle,<br>This earth of majesty, this seat of Mars,<br>This other Eden, demi-paradise,<br>This fortress built by [[Nature\|nature]] for herself<br>Against infection and the hand of war,<br>This happy breed of men, this little world,<br>This precious stone set in the silver sea,<br>Which serves it in the office of a wall,<br>Or as a moat defensive to a [[house]]<br>Against the envy of less happier lands;<br>This blessèd plot, this earth, this realm, this England,<br>This [[Nurse\|nurse]], this teeming womb of royal kings,<br>Feared by their breed and famous by their birth,<br>Renowned for their deeds as far from [[Home\|home]]<br>For Christian service and true chivalry<br>As is the sepulchre, in stubborn Jewry,<br>Of the world’s ransom, blessed Mary’s son;<br>This land of such dear souls, this dear dear land,<br>Dear for her reputation through the world,<br>Is now leased out—I die pronouncing it—<br>Like to a tenement or pelting farm.<br>England, bound in with the triumphant sea,<br>Whose rocky shore beats back the envious siege<br>Of wat’ry Neptune, is now bound in with shame,<br>With inky blots and rotten parchment bonds.<br>That England that was wont to conquer others<br>Hath made a shameful conquest of itself.<br>Ah, would the scandal vanish with my life,<br>How happy then were my ensuing [[Death\|death]]! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] [[Richard]] and the [[Queen]]; the [[Duke of Aumerle]], Bushy, Green, Bagot, Lord Ross, and Lord Willoughby |
| YORK | The [[King]] is come. Deal mildly with his youth,<br>For young hot colts, being reined, do rage the more. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | How fares our noble uncle Lancaster? |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | What comfort, man? How is’t with aged Gaunt? |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | O, how that name befits my composition I<br>Old Gaunt indeed, and gaunt in being old. |
| *(didascalia)* | Within me grief hath kept a tedious fast, |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | And who abstains from meat that is not gaunt?<br>For sleeping England long [[Time\|time]] have I watched.<br>Watching breeds leanness, leanness is all gaunt.<br>The pleasure that some fathers feed upon<br>Is my strict fast: I mean my children’s looks.<br>And therein fasting, hast thou made me gaunt.<br>Gaunt am I for the [[Grave\|grave]], gaunt as a [[Grave\|grave]],<br>Whose hollow womb inherits naught but bones. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Can sick men play so nicely with their names? |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | No, misery makes sport to mock itself.<br>Since thou dost seek to kill my name in me,<br>I mock my name, great [[King]], to flatter thee. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Should dying men flatter with those that live? |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | No, no, men living flatter those that die. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Thou now a-dying sayst thou flatt’rest me. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | O no: thou diest, though I the sicker be. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | I am in health; I breathe, and see thee ill. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | Now He that made me knows I see thee ill:<br>Ill in myself to see, and in thee seeing ill.<br>Thy deathbed is no lesser than thy land,<br>Wherein thou liest in reputation sick;<br>And thou, too careless patient as thou art,<br>Committ’st thy anointed [[Body\|body]] to the cure<br>Of those physicians that first wounded thee.<br>A thousand flatterers sit within thy [[Crown\|crown]],<br>Whose compass is no bigger than thy head,<br>And yet, encagèd in so small a verge,<br>The waste is no whit lesser than thy land.<br>O, had thy grandsire with a [[Prophet\|prophet]]’s eye<br>Seen how his son’s son should destroy his sons,<br>From forth thy reach he would have laid thy shame,<br>Deposing thee before thou wert possessed,<br>Which art possessed now to depose thyself.<br>Why, cousin, wert thou regent of the world<br>It were a shame to let this land by lease.<br>But, for thy world, enjoying but this land,<br>Is it not more than shame to shame it so?<br>Landlord of England art thou now, not [[King\|king]].<br>Thy state of law is bondslave to the law,<br>And— |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | And thou, a lunatic lean-witted [[Fool\|fool]],<br>Presuming on an ague’s privilege,<br>Dar’st with thy frozen admonition<br>Make pale our cheek, chasing the royal [[Blood\|blood]]<br>With fury from his native residence.<br>Now by my seat’s right royal majesty,<br>Wert thou not brother to great Edward’s son,<br>This tongue that runs so roundly in thy head<br>Should run thy head from thy unreverent shoulders. |
| [[John of Gaunt\|JOHN OF GAUNT]] | O, spare me not, my brother Edward’s son,<br>For that I was his father Edward’s son.<br>That [[Blood\|blood]] already, like the pelican,<br>Hast thou tapped out and drunkenly caroused.<br>My brother [[Gloucester]], plain [[Well\|well]]-meaning [[Soul\|soul]]—<br>Whom fair befall in heaven ‘mongst happy souls—<br>May be a precedent and witness good<br>That thou respect’st not spilling Edward’s [[Blood\|blood]].<br>Join with the present sickness that I have,<br>And thy unkindness be like crooked age,<br>To crop at once a too-long withered flower.<br>Live in thy shame, but die not shame with thee.<br>These words hereafter thy tormentors be.<br>(To attendants) Convey me to my bed, then to my [[Grave\|grave]].<br>[[Love]] they to live that [[Love\|love]] and [[Honour\|honour]] have. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit, [carried in the chair] |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | And let them die that age and sullens have,<br>For both hast thou, and both become the [[Grave\|grave]]. |
| YORK | I do beseech your majesty impute his words<br>To wayward sickliness and age in him.<br>He loves you, on my life, and holds you dear<br>As Harry Duke of Hereford, were he here. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Right, you say true: as Hereford’s [[Love\|love]], so his.<br>As theirs, so mine; and all be as it is. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Northumberland]] |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | My liege, old Gaunt commends him to your majesty. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | What says he? |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Nay, nothing: all is said.<br>His tongue is now a stringless instrument.<br>Words, life, and all, old Lancaster hath spent. |
| YORK | Be York the next that must be bankrupt so!<br>Though [[Death\|death]] be poor, it ends a mortal woe. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | The ripest fruit first falls, and so doth he.<br>His [[Time\|time]] is spent; our pilgrimage must be.<br>So much for that. Now for our Irish wars.<br>We must supplant those rough rug-headed kerns,<br>Which live like venom where no venom else<br>But only they have privilege to live.<br>And for these great affairs do ask some charge,<br>Towards our assistance we do seize to us<br>The plate, coin, revenues, and movables<br>Whereof our uncle Gaunt did stand possessed. |
| YORK | How long shall I be patient? Ah, how long<br>Shall tender duty make me suffer wrong?<br>Not [[Gloucester]]’s [[Death\|death]], nor Hereford’s banishment,<br>Nor Gaunt’s rebukes, nor England’s private wrongs,<br>Nor the prevention of poor Bolingbroke<br>About his [[Marriage\|marriage]], nor my own disgrace,<br>Have ever made me sour my patient cheek,<br>Or bend one wrinkle on my sovereign’s face.<br>I am the last of noble Edward’s sons,<br>Of whom thy father, Prince of Wales, was first.<br>In war was never [[Lion\|lion]] raged more fierce,<br>In peace was never gentle [[Lamb\|lamb]] more mild,<br>Than was that young and princely gentleman.<br>His face thou hast, for even so looked he,<br>Accomplished with the number of thy hours.<br>But when he frowned it was against the French,<br>And not against his friends. His noble hand<br>Did win what he did spend, and spent not that<br>Which his triumphant father’s hand had won.<br>His hands were guilty of no kindred [[Blood\|blood]],<br>But bloody with the enemies of his kin.<br>O, [[Richard]], York is too far gone with grief,<br>Or else he never would compare between. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Why uncle, what’s the matter? |
| YORK | O my liege,<br>Pardon me if you please; if not, I, pleased<br>Not to be pardoned, am content withal.<br>Seek you to seize and grip into your hands<br>The royalties and rights of banished Hereford?<br>Is not Gaunt dead? And doth not Hereford live?<br>Was not Gaunt just? And is not Harry true?<br>Did not the one deserve to have an heir?<br>Is not his heir a [[Well\|well]]-deserving son?<br>Take Hereford’s rights away, and take from [[Time]]<br>His charters and his customary rights:<br>Let not tomorrow then ensue today;<br>Be not thyself, for how art thou a [[King\|king]]<br>But by fair sequence and succession?<br>Now afore God—God forbid I say true!—<br>If you do wrongfully seize Hereford’s rights,<br>Call in the letters patents that he hath<br>By his attorneys general to sue<br>His livery, and deny his offered homage,<br>You pluck a thousand dangers on your head,<br>You lose a thousand [[Well\|well]]-disposed hearts,<br>And prick my tender patience to those thoughts<br>Which [[Honour\|honour]] and allegiance cannot think. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Think what you will, we seize into our hands<br>His plate, his goods, his money, and his lands. |
| YORK | I’ll not be by the while. My liege, farewell.<br>What will ensue hereof there’s none can tell.<br>But by bad courses may be understood<br>That their events can never fall out good. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Go, Bushy, to the Earl of Wiltshire straight.<br>Bid him repair to us to Ely [[house\|House]]<br>To see this business. Tomorrow next<br>We will for Ireland, and ’tis [[Time\|time]], I trow.<br>And we create, in absence of ourself,<br>Our uncle York Lord Governor of England;<br>For he is just and always loved us [[Well\|well]].—<br>Come on, our [[Queen]]; tomorrow must we part.<br>Be merry, for our [[Time\|time]] of stay is short. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt Bushy at one door; [[King]] [[Richard]], the [[Queen]], Aumerle, Green, and Bagot at another door. Northumberland, Willoughby, and Ross remain |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | [[Well]], lords, the Duke of Lancaster is dead. |
| ROSS | And living too, for now his son is Duke. |
| WILLOUGHBY | Barely in title, not in revenues. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Richly in both, if justice had her right. |
| ROSS | My heart is great, but it must break with [[Silence\|silence]]<br>Ere’t be disburdened with a liberal tongue. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Nay, speak thy mind, and let him ne’er speak more<br>That speaks thy words again to do thee harm. |
| WILLOUGHBY | Tends that that thou wouldst speak to the Duke of Hereford?<br>If it be so, out with it boldly, man.<br>Quick is mine ear to hear of good towards him. |
| ROSS | No good at all that I can do for him,<br>Unless you call it good to pity him,<br>Bereft and gelded of his patrimony. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Now afore God, ‘tis shame such wrongs are borne<br>In him, a royal prince, and many more<br>Of noble [[Blood\|blood]] in this declining land.<br>The [[King]] is not himself, but basely led<br>By flatterers; and what they will inform<br>Merely in hate ’gainst any of us all,<br>That will the [[King]] severely prosecute<br>’Gainst us, our lives, our children, and our heirs. |
| ROSS | The commons hath he pilled with grievous taxes,<br>And quite lost their hearts. The nobles hath he fined<br>For ancient quarrels, and quite lost their hearts. |
| WILLOUGHBY | And daily new exactions are devised,<br>As blanks, benevolences, and I wot not what.<br>But what, a’ God’s name, doth become of this? |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Wars hath not wasted it; for warred he hath not,<br>But basely yielded upon compromise<br>That which his ancestors achieved with blows.<br>More hath he spent in peace than they in wars. |
| ROSS | The Earl of Wiltshire hath the realm in farm. |
| WILLOUGHBY | The [[King]]’s grown bankrupt like a broken man. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Reproach and dissolution hangeth over him. |
| ROSS | He hath not money for these Irish wars,<br>His burdenous taxations notwithstanding,<br>But by the robbing of the banished Duke. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | His noble kinsman. Most degenerate [[King]]!<br>But, lords, we hear this fearful tempest sing,<br>Yet seek no shelter to avoid the [[Storm\|storm]].<br>We see the [[Wind\|wind]] sit sore upon our sails,<br>And yet we strike not, but securely perish. |
| ROSS | We see the very wreck that we must suffer,<br>And unavoided is the danger now<br>For [[Suffering\|suffering]] so the causes of our wreck. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Not so: even through the hollow eyes of [[Death\|death]]<br>I spy life peering; but I dare not say<br>How near the tidings of our comfort is. |
| WILLOUGHBY | Nay, let us share thy thoughts, as thou dost ours. |
| ROSS | Be confident to speak, Northumberland.<br>We three are but thyself, and, speaking so,<br>Thy words are but as thoughts. Therefore be bold. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Then thus. I have from Port le Blanc,<br>A bay in Brittaine, received intelligence<br>That Harry Duke of Hereford, Reinold Lord Cobham,<br>Thomas son and heir to the Earl of Arundel<br>That late broke from the [[Duke of Exeter]],<br>His brother, Archbishop late of Canterbury,<br>Sir Thomas Erpingham, Sir Thomas Ramston,<br>Sir John Norbery,<br>Sir Robert Waterton, and Francis Coint,<br>All these [[Well\|well]] furnished by the Duke of Brittaine<br>With eight tall ships, three thousand men of war,<br>Are making hither with all due expedience,<br>And shortly mean to touch our northern shore.<br>Perhaps they had ere this, but that they stay<br>The first departing of the [[King]] for Ireland.<br>If then we shall shake off our slavish yoke,<br>Imp out our drooping country’s broken wing,<br>Redeem from broking pawn the blemished [[Crown\|crown]],<br>Wipe off the [[Dust\|dust]] that hides our sceptre’s gilt,<br>And make high majesty look like itself,<br>Away with me in post to Ravenspurgh.<br>But if you faint, as fearing to do so,<br>Stay, and be secret, and myself will go. |
| ROSS | To [[Horse\|horse]], to [[Horse\|horse]]! Urge doubts to them that [[Fear\|fear]]. |
| WILLOUGHBY | Hold out my [[Horse\|horse]], and I will first be there. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Queen]], Bushy, and Bagot |
| BUSHY | Madam, your majesty is too much sad.<br>You promised when you parted with the [[King]]<br>To lay aside life-harming heaviness<br>And entertain a cheerful disposition. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | To please the [[King]] I did; to please myself<br>I cannot do it. Yet I know no cause<br>Why I should welcome such a guest as grief,<br>Save bidding farewell to so sweet a guest<br>As my sweet [[Richard]]. Yet again, methinks<br>Some [[unborn]] sorrow, ripe in fortune’s womb,<br>Is coming towards me; and my inward [[Soul\|soul]]<br>At nothing trembles. With something it grieves<br>More than with parting from my lord the [[King]]. |
| BUSHY | Each substance of a grief hath twenty shadows<br>Which shows like grief itself but is not so.<br>For sorrow’s eye, glazed with blinding [[Tears\|tears]],<br>Divides one thing entire to many objects—<br>Like perspectives, which, rightly gazed upon,<br>Show nothing but confusion; eyed awry,<br>Distinguish form. So your sweet majesty,<br>Looking awry upon your lord’s departure,<br>Find shapes of grief more than himself to wail,<br>Which, looked on as it is, is naught but shadows<br>Of what it is not. Then, thrice-gracious [[Queen]],<br>More than your lord’s departure weep not: more is not seen,<br>Or if it be, ’tis with false sorrow’s eye,<br>Which for things true weeps things imaginary. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | It may be so, but yet my inward [[Soul\|soul]]<br>Persuades me it is otherwise. Howe’er it be,<br>I cannot but be sad: so heavy-sad<br>As thought—on thinking on no thought I think—<br>Makes me with heavy nothing faint and shrink. |
| BUSHY | ’Tis nothing but conceit, my gracious lady. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | ‘Tis nothing less: conceit is still derived<br>From some forefather grief; mine is not so;<br>For nothing hath begot my something grief—<br>Or something hath the nothing that I grieve—<br>’Tis in reversion that I do possess—<br>But what it is that is not yet known what,<br>I cannot name; ’tis nameless woe, I wot. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Green |
| GREEN | God save your majesty, and [[Well\|well]] met, gentlemen.<br>I [[Hope\|hope]] the [[King]] is not yet shipped for Ireland. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Why hop‘st thou so? ’Tis better [[Hope\|hope]] he is,<br>For his designs crave haste, his haste good [[Hope\|hope]].<br>Then wherefore dost thou [[Hope\|hope]] he is not shipped? |
| GREEN | That he, our [[Hope\|hope]], might have retired his power,<br>And driven into [[Despair\|despair]] an enemy’s [[Hope\|hope]],<br>Who strongly hath set footing in this land.<br>The banished Bolingbroke repeals himself,<br>And with uplifted arms is safe arrived<br>At Ravenspurgh. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Now God in heaven forbid! |
| GREEN | Ah madam, ’tis too true! And, that is worse,<br>The Lord Northumberland, his son young Harry Percy,<br>The Lords of Ross, Beaumont, and Willoughby,<br>With all their powerful friends, are fled to him. |
| BUSHY | Why have you not proclaimed Northumberland,<br>And all the rest, revolted faction-traitors? |
| GREEN | We have; whereupon the [[Earl of Worcester]]<br>Hath broke his staff, resigned his stewardship,<br>And all the household servants fled with him<br>To Bolingbroke. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | So, Green, thou art the midwife to my woe,<br>And Bolingbroke my sorrow’s dismal heir.<br>Now hath my [[Soul\|soul]] brought forth her prodigy,<br>And I, a gasping new-delivered mother,<br>Have woe to woe, sorrow to sorrow joined. |
| BUSHY | [[Despair]] not, madam. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Who shall hinder me?<br>I will [[Despair\|despair]], and be at enmity<br>With cozening [[Hope\|hope]]. He is a flatterer,<br>A parasite, a keeper-back of [[Death\|death]],<br>Who gently would dissolve the bonds of life,<br>Which false [[Hope\|hope]] lingers in extremity. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Duke of York]], wearing a gorget |
| GREEN | Here comes the [[Duke of York]]. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | With signs of war about his aged neck.<br>O, full of careful business are his looks!<br>Uncle, for God’s sake speak comfortable words. |
| YORK | Should I do so, I should belie my thoughts.<br>Comfort’s in heaven, and we are on the earth,<br>Where nothing lives but crosses, cares, and grief.<br>Your husband, he is gone to save far off,<br>Whilst others come to make him lose at [[Home\|home]].<br>Here am I, left to underprop his land,<br>Who, weak with age, cannot support myself.<br>Now comes the sick hour that his surfeit made.<br>Now shall he try his friends that flattered him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Servingman |
| SERVINGMAN | My lord, your son was gone before I came. |
| YORK | He was? Why so, go all which way it will.<br>The nobles they are fled. The commons they are cold,<br>And will, I [[Fear\|fear]], revolt on Hereford’s side.<br>Sirrah, get thee to Pleshey, to my sister [[Gloucester]].<br>Bid her send me presently a thousand pound—<br>Hold; take my ring. |
| SERVINGMAN | My lord, I had forgot to tell your lordship,<br>Today as I came by I called there—<br>But I shall grieve you to report the rest. |
| YORK | What is’t, knave? |
| SERVINGMAN | An hour before I came, the Duchess died. |
| YORK | God for his [[Mercy\|mercy]], what a tide of woes<br>Comes rushing on this woeful land at once!<br>I know not what to do. I would to God,<br>So my untruth had not provoked him to it,<br>The [[King]] had cut off my head with my brother’s.<br>What, are there no posts dispatched for Ireland?<br>How shall we do for money for these wars?<br>(To the [[Queen]]) Come, sister—cousin, I would say; pray<br>pardon me.<br>(To the Servingman) Go, fellow, get thee [[Home\|home]]. Provide<br>some carts,<br>And bring away the armour that is there. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Servingman |
| YORK | Gentlemen, will you go muster men?<br>If I know how or which way to order these affairs<br>Thus disorderly thrust into my hands,<br>Never believe me. Both are my kinsmen.<br>T‘one is my sovereign, whom both my oath<br>And duty bids defend; t’other again<br>Is my kinsman, whom the [[King]] hath wronged,<br>Whom [[Conscience\|conscience]] and my kindred bids to right.<br>[[Well]], somewhat we must do. (To the [[Queen]]) Come,<br>cousin,<br>I’ll dispose of you.—<br>Gentlemen, go muster up your men,<br>And meet me presently at Berkeley [[Castle]].<br>I should to Pleshey too, but [[Time\|time]] will not permit.<br>All is uneven,<br>And everything is left at six and seven. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the [[Duke of York]] and the [[Queen]]. Bushy, Bagot, and Green remain |
| BUSHY | The [[Wind\|wind]] sits fair for news to go for Ireland,<br>But none returns. For us to levy power<br>Proportionable to the enemy<br>Is all unpossible. |
| GREEN | Besides, our nearness to the [[King]] in [[Love\|love]]<br>Is near the hate of those [[Love\|love]] not the [[King]]. |
| BAGOT | And that is the wavering commons; for their [[Love\|love]]<br>Lies in their purses, and whoso empties them<br>By so much fills their hearts with deadly hate. |
| BUSHY | Wherein the [[King]] stands generally condemned. |
| BAGOT | If judgement lie in them, then so do we,<br>Because we ever have been near the [[King]]. |
| GREEN | [[Well]], I will for refuge straight to Bristol [[Castle]].<br>The Earl of Wiltshire is already there. |
| BUSHY | Thither will I with you; for little office<br>Will the hateful commoners perform for us,<br>Except like curs to tear us all to pieces.<br>(To Bagot) Will you go along with us? |
| BAGOT | No, I will to Ireland, to his majesty.<br>Farewell: if heart’s presages be not vain<br>We three here part that ne’er shall meet again. |
| BUSHY | That’s as York thrives to beat back Bolingbroke. |
| GREEN | Alas, poor Duke, the task he undertakes<br>Is numb’ring sands and drinking oceans dry.<br>Where one on his side fights, thousands will fly. |
| *(didascalia)* | BAGOT |
| GREEN | Farewell at once, for once, for all and ever. |
| BUSHY | [[Well]], we may meet again. |
| BAGOT | I [[Fear\|fear]] me never. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Bushy and Green at one door, and Bagot at another door |

### Atto 2, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bolingbroke Duke of Lancaster and Hereford, and the [[Earl of Northumberland]] |
| BOLINGBROKE | How far is it, my lord, to Berkeley now? |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Believe me, noble lord,<br>I am a stranger here in Gloucestershire.<br>These high wild hills and rough uneven ways<br>Draws out our miles and makes them wearisome;<br>And yet your fair discourse hath been as sugar,<br>Making the hard way sweet and delectable.<br>But I bethink me what a weary way<br>From Ravenspurgh to Cotswold will be found<br>In Ross and Willoughby, wanting your company,<br>Which I protest hath very much beguiled<br>The tediousness and process of my travel.<br>But theirs is sweetened with the [[Hope\|hope]] to have<br>The present benefit which I possess;<br>And [[Hope\|hope]] to joy is little less in joy<br>Than [[Hope\|hope]] enjoyed. By this the weary lords<br>Shall make their way seem short as mine hath done<br>By sight of what I have: your noble company. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Of much less value is my company<br>Than your good words. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Harry Percy |
| BOLINGBROKE | But who comes here? |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | It is my son, young Harry Percy,<br>Sent from my brother Worcester, whencesoever.<br>Harry, how fares your uncle? |
| HARRY PERCY | I had thought, my lord, to have learned his health of<br>you. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Why, is he not with the [[Queen]]? |
| HARRY PERCY | No, my good lord; he hath forsook the [[Court\|court]],<br>Broken his staff of office, and dispersed<br>The household of the [[King]]. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | What was his reason? He was not so resolved when last we spake together. |
| HARRY PERCY | Because your lordship was proclaimed traitor.<br>But he, my lord, is gone to Ravenspurgh<br>To offer service to the Duke of Hereford,<br>And sent me over by Berkeley to discover<br>What power the [[Duke of York]] had levied there,<br>Then with directions to repair to Ravenspurgh. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Have you forgot the Duke of Hereford, boy? |
| HARRY PERCY | No, my good lord, for that is not forgot<br>Which ne’er I did remember. To my knowledge,<br>I never in my life did look on him. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Then learn to know him now. This is the Duke. |
| HARRY PERCY | My gracious lord, I tender you my service,<br>Such as it is, being tender, raw, and young,<br>Which elder days shall ripen and confirm<br>To more approved service and [[Desert\|desert]]. |
| BOLINGBROKE | I thank thee, gentle Percy, and be sure<br>I count myself in nothing else so happy<br>As in a [[Soul\|soul]] rememb’ring my good friends;<br>And as my fortune ripens with thy [[Love\|love]],<br>It shall be still thy true [[Love\|love]]’s recompense.<br>My heart this covenant makes; my hand thus seals it.<br>He gives Percy his hand |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | How far is it to Berkeley, and what stir<br>Keeps good old York there with his men of war? |
| HARRY PERCY | There stands the [[Castle\|castle]], by yon tuft of trees,<br>Manned with three hundred men, as I have heard,<br>And in it are the Lords of York, Berkeley, and Seymour,<br>None else of name and noble estimate. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lord Ross and Lord Willoughby |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Here come the Lords of Ross and Willoughby,<br>Bloody with spurring, fiery red with haste. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Welcome, my lords. I wot your [[Love\|love]] pursues<br>A banished traitor. All my treasury<br>Is yet but unfelt thanks, which, more enriched,<br>Shall be your [[Love\|love]] and labour’s recompense. |
| ROSS | Your presence makes us rich, most noble lord. |
| WILLOUGHBY | And far surmounts our labour to attain it. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Evermore thank’s the exchequer of the poor,<br>Which till my infant fortune comes to years<br>Stands for my bounty. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Berkeley |
| BOLINGBROKE | But who comes here? |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | It is my lord of Berkeley, as I guess. |
| BERKELEY | My lord of Hereford, my message is to you. |
| BOLINGBROKE | My lord, my answer is to ‘Lancaster’,<br>And I am come to seek that name in England,<br>And I must find that title in your tongue<br>Before I make reply to aught you say. |
| BERKELEY | Mistake me not, my lord, ’tis not my meaning<br>To raze one title of your [[Honour\|honour]] out.<br>To you, my lord, I come—what lord you will—<br>From the most gracious regent of this land,<br>The [[Duke of York]], to know what pricks you on<br>To take advantage of the absent [[Time\|time]]<br>And fright our native peace with [[Self\|self]]-borne arms. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Duke of York]] |
| BOLINGBROKE | I shall not need transport my words by you.<br>Here comes his grace in person.—My noble uncle!<br>He kneels |
| YORK | Show me thy humble heart, and not thy knee,<br>Whose duty is deceivable and false. |
| BOLINGBROKE | My gracious uncle— |
| YORK | Tut, tut, grace me no grace, nor uncle me no uncle.<br>I am no traitor’s uncle, and that word ‘grace’<br>In an ungracious mouth is but profane.<br>Why have those banished and forbidden legs<br>Dared once to touch a [[Dust\|dust]] of England’s ground?<br>But then more ‘why’: why have they dared to march<br>So many miles upon her peaceful bosom,<br>Frighting her pale-faced villages with war<br>And ostentation of despised arms?<br>Com’st thou because the anointed [[King]] is hence?<br>Why, foolish boy, the [[King]] is left behind,<br>And in my loyal bosom lies his power.<br>Were I but now the lord of such hot youth<br>As when brave Gaunt, thy father, and myself<br>Rescued the Black Prince, that young Mars of men,<br>From forth the ranks of many thousand French,<br>O then how quickly should this arm of mine,<br>Now prisoner to the palsy, chastise thee<br>And minister correction to thy fault! |
| BOLINGBROKE | My gracious uncle, let me know my fault.<br>On what condition stands it and wherein? |
| YORK | Even in condition of the worst degree:<br>In gross rebellion and detested treason.<br>Thou art a banished man, and here art come<br>Before the expiration of thy [[Time\|time]]<br>In braving arms against thy sovereign. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOLINGBROKE standing |
| YORK | As I was banished, I was banished Hereford;<br>But as I come, I come for Lancaster.<br>And, noble uncle, I beseech your grace,<br>Look on my wrongs with an indifferent eye.<br>You are my father, for methinks in you<br>I see old Gaunt alive. O then, my father,<br>Will you permit that I shall stand condemned<br>A wandering vagabond, my rights and royalties<br>Plucked from my arms perforce and given away<br>To upstart unthrifts? Wherefore was I born?<br>If that my cousin [[King]] be [[King]] in England,<br>It must be granted I am Duke of Lancaster.<br>You have a son, Aumerle my noble kinsman.<br>Had you first died and he been thus trod down,<br>He should have found his uncle Gaunt a father<br>To rouse his wrongs and chase them to the bay.<br>I am denied to sue my livery here,<br>And yet my letters patents give me leave.<br>My father’s goods are all distrained and sold,<br>And these and all are all amiss employed.<br>What would you have me do? I am a subject,<br>And I challenge law; attorneys are denied me;<br>And therefore personally I lay my claim<br>To my inheritance of free descent. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | The noble Duke hath been too much abused. |
| ROSS | It stands your grace upon to do him right. |
| WILLOUGHBY | Base men by his endowments are made great. |
| YORK | My lords of England, let me tell you this.<br>I have had feeling of my cousin’s wrongs,<br>And laboured all I could to do him right.<br>But in this kind to come, in braving arms,<br>Be his own carver, and cut out his way<br>To find out right with wrong—it may not be.<br>And you that do abet him in this kind<br>Cherish rebellion, and are rebels all. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | The noble Duke hath sworn his coming is<br>But for his own, and for the right of that<br>We all have strongly sworn to give him aid;<br>And let him never see joy that breaks that oath. |
| YORK | [[Well]], [[Well\|well]], I see the issue of these arms.<br>I cannot mend it, I must needs confess,<br>Because my power is weak and all ill-left.<br>But if I could, by Him that gave me life,<br>I would attach you all, and make you stoop<br>Unto the sovereign [[Mercy\|mercy]] of the [[King]].<br>But since I cannot, be it known to you<br>I do remain as neuter. So fare you [[Well\|well]]—<br>Unless you please to enter in the [[Castle\|castle]]<br>And there repose you for this [[Night\|night]]. |
| BOLINGBROKE | An offer, uncle, that we will accept.<br>But we must win your grace to go with us<br>To Bristol [[Castle]], which they say is held<br>By Bushy, Bagot, and their complices,<br>The caterpillars of the commonwealth,<br>Which I have sworn to weed and pluck away. |
| YORK | It may be I will go with you—but yet I’ll pause,<br>For I am loath to break our country’s laws.<br>Nor friends nor foes, to me welcome you are.<br>Things past redress are now with me past care. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Salisbury]] and a Welsh Captain |
| WELSH CAPTAIN | My lord of Salisbury, we have stayed ten days,<br>And hardly kept our countrymen together,<br>And yet we hear no tidings from the [[King]].<br>Therefore we will disperse ourselves. Farewell. |
| SALISBURY | Stay yet another day, thou trusty Welshman.<br>The [[King]] reposeth all his confidence in thee. |
| WELSH CAPTAIN | ’Tis thought the [[King]] is dead. We will not stay.<br>The bay trees in our country are all withered,<br>And meteors fright the fixèd [[Stars\|stars]] of heaven.<br>The pale-faced [[Moon\|moon]] looks bloody on the earth,<br>And lean-looked prophets whisper fearful change.<br>Rich men look sad, and ruffians [[Dance\|dance]] and leap;<br>The one in [[Fear\|fear]] to lose what they enjoy,<br>The other to enjoy by rage and war.<br>These signs forerun the [[Death\|death]] or fall of kings.<br>Farewell. Our countrymen are gone and fled,<br>As [[Well\|well]] assured [[Richard]] their [[King\|king]] is dead. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| SALISBURY | Ah, [[Richard]]! With the eyes of heavy mind<br>I see thy glory, like a shooting star,<br>Fall to the base earth from the firmament.<br>Thy sun sets weeping in the lowly west,<br>Witnessing storms to come, woe, and unrest.<br>Thy friends are fled to wait upon thy foes,<br>And crossly to thy good all fortune goes. Exit |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bolingbroke Duke of Lancaster and Hereford, the [[Duke of York]], the [[Earl of Northumberland]], Lord Ross, Harry Percy, and Lord Willoughby |
| BOLINGBROKE | Bring forth these men. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bushy and Green, guarded as prisoners |
| BOLINGBROKE | Bushy and Green, I will not vex your souls,<br>Since presently your souls must part your bodies,<br>With too much urging your pernicious lives,<br>For ’twere no [[Charity\|charity]]. Yet to wash your [[Blood\|blood]]<br>From off my hands, here in the view of men<br>I will unfold some causes of your deaths.<br>You have misled a prince, a royal [[King\|king]],<br>A happy gentleman in [[Blood\|blood]] and lineaments,<br>By you unhappied and disfigured clean.<br>You have, in manner, with your sinful hours<br>Made a [[divorce]] betwixt his [[Queen\|queen]] and him,<br>Broke the possession of a royal bed,<br>And stained the [[Beauty\|beauty]] of a fair [[Queen\|queen]]’s cheeks<br>With [[Tears\|tears]] drawn from her eyes by your foul wrongs.<br>Myself—a prince by fortune of my birth,<br>Near to the [[King]] in [[Blood\|blood]], and near in [[Love\|love]]<br>Till you did make him misinterpret me—<br>Have stooped my neck under your injuries,<br>And sighed my English breath in foreign clouds,<br>Eating the bitter bread of banishment,<br>Whilst you have fed upon my signories,<br>Disparked my parks and felled my forest woods,<br>From my own windows torn my household coat,<br>Razed out my imprese, leaving me no sign,<br>Save men’s opinions and my living [[Blood\|blood]],<br>To show the world I am a gentleman.<br>This and much more, much more than twice all this,<br>Condemns you to the [[Death\|death]].—See them delivered over<br>To execution and the hand of [[Death\|death]]. |
| BUSHY | More welcome is the stroke of [[Death\|death]] to me<br>Than Bolingbroke to England. |
| GREEN | My comfort is that heaven will take our souls,<br>And plague injustice with the pains of hell. |
| BOLINGBROKE | My lord Northumberland, see them dispatched. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Northumberland, with Bushy and Green, guarded |
| BOLINGBROKE | Uncle, you say the [[Queen]] is at your [[house]].<br>For God’s sake, fairly let her be intreated.<br>Tell her I send to her my kind commends.<br>Take special care my greetings be delivered. |
| YORK | A gentleman of mine I have dispatched<br>With letters of your [[Love\|love]] to her at large. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Thanks, gentle uncle.—Come, lords, away,<br>To fight with Glyndwr and his complices.<br>A while to work, and after, holiday. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 3.2 Flourish. Enter [[King]] [[Richard]], the [[Duke of Aumerle]], the Bishop of Carlisle, and soldiers, with drum and colours |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Harlechly [[Castle]] call they this at hand? |
| AUMERLE | Yea, my lord. How brooks your grace the air<br>After your late tossing on the breaking seas? |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Needs must I like it [[Well\|well]]. I weep for joy<br>To stand upon my kingdom once again.<br>He touches the ground<br>Dear earth, I do salute thee with my hand,<br>Though rebels wound thee with their horses’ hoofs.<br>As a long-parted mother with her [[Child\|child]]<br>Plays fondly with her [[Tears\|tears]], and smiles in meeting,<br>So, weeping, smiling, greet I thee my earth,<br>And do thee favours with my royal hands.<br>Feed not thy sovereign’s foe, my gentle earth,<br>Nor with thy sweets comfort his ravenous sense;<br>But let thy spiders that suck up thy venom<br>And heavy-gaited toads lie in their way,<br>Doing annoyance to the treacherous feet<br>Which with usurping steps do trample thee.<br>Yield stinging nettles to mine enemies,<br>And when they from thy bosom pluck a flower<br>Guard it, I pray thee, with a lurking adder,<br>Whose [[Double\|double]] tongue may with a mortal touch<br>Throw [[Death\|death]] upon thy sovereign’s enemies.—<br>Mock not my senseless conjuration, lords.<br>This earth shall have a feeling, and these stones<br>Prove armed soldiers, ere her native [[King\|king]]<br>Shall falter under foul rebellion’s arms. |
| BISHOP OF CARLISLE | [[Fear]] not, my lord. That power that made you [[King\|king]]<br>Hath power to keep you [[King\|king]] in spite of all. |
| AUMERLE | He means, my lord, that we are too remiss,<br>Whilst Bolingbroke, through our security,<br>Grows strong and great in substance and in friends. |
