| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Discomfortable cousin, know‘st thou not<br>That when the searching eye of heaven is hid<br>Behind the globe, that lights the lower world,<br>Then thieves and robbers range abroad unseen<br>In murders and in outrage bloody here;<br>But when from under this terrestrial ball<br>He fires the proud tops of the eastern pines,<br>And darts his light through every guilty hole,<br>Then murders, treasons, and detested sins,<br>The cloak of [[Night\|night]] being plucked from off their backs,<br>Stand bare and naked, trembling at themselves?<br>So when this thief, this traitor, Bolingbroke,<br>Who all this while hath revelled in the [[Night\|night]]<br>Whilst we were wand’ring with the Antipodes,<br>Shall see us rising in our throne, the east,<br>His treasons will sit blushing in his face,<br>Not able to endure the sight of day,<br>But, [[Self\|self]]-affrighted, tremble at his sin.<br>Not all the [[Water\|water]] in the rough rude sea<br>Can wash the balm from an anointed [[King\|king]].<br>The breath of worldly men cannot depose<br>The deputy elected by the Lord.<br>For every man that Bolingbroke hath pressed<br>To lift shrewd steel against our golden [[Crown\|crown]],<br>God for his [[Richard]] hath in heavenly pay<br>A glorious angel. Then if angels fight,<br>Weak men must fall; for heaven still guards the right. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Salisbury]] |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Welcome, my lord. How far off lies your power? |
| SALISBURY | Nor nea’er nor farther off, my gracious lord,<br>Than this weak arm. Discomfort guides my tongue,<br>And bids me speak of nothing but [[Despair\|despair]].<br>One day too late, I [[Fear\|fear]] me, noble lord,<br>Hath clouded all thy happy days on earth.<br>O, call back yesterday, bid [[Time\|time]] return,<br>And thou shalt have twelve thousand fighting men.<br>Today, today, unhappy day too late,<br>Overthrows thy joys, friends, fortune, and thy state;<br>For all the Welshmen, hearing thou wert dead,<br>Are gone to Bolingbroke, dispersed, and fled. |
| AUMERLE | Comfort, my liege. Why looks your grace so pale? |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | But now the [[Blood\|blood]] of twenty thousand men<br>Did triumph in my face, and they are fled;<br>And till so much [[Blood\|blood]] thither come again<br>Have I not reason to look pale and dead?<br>All souls that will be safe fly from my side,<br>For [[Time\|time]] hath set a blot upon my [[Pride\|pride]]. |
| AUMERLE | Comfort, my liege. Remember who you are. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | I had forgot myself. Am I not [[King]]?<br>Awake, thou sluggard majesty, thou [[Sleep\|sleep]]’st!<br>Is not the [[King]]’s name forty thousand names?<br>Arm, arm, my name! A puny subject strikes<br>At thy great glory. Look not to the ground,<br>Ye favourites of a [[King\|king]]: are we not high?<br>High be our thoughts. I know my uncle York<br>Hath power enough to serve our turn. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Scrope |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | But who comes here? |
| SCROPE | More health and happiness betide my liege<br>Than can my care-tuned tongue deliver him. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Mine ear is open and my heart prepared.<br>The worst is worldly loss thou canst unfold.<br>Say, is my kingdom lost? Why ’twas my care,<br>And what loss is it to be rid of care?<br>Strives Bolingbroke to be as great as we?<br>Greater he shall not be. If he serve God<br>We’ll serve Him too, and be his fellow so.<br>Revolt our subjects? That we cannot mend.<br>They break their [[Faith\|faith]] to God as [[Well\|well]] as us.<br>Cry woe, destruction, ruin, loss, [[Decay\|decay]]:<br>The worst is [[Death\|death]], and [[Death\|death]] will have his day. |
| SCROPE | Glad am I that your highness is so armed<br>To bear the tidings of calamity.<br>Like an unseasonable stormy day,<br>Which makes the silver rivers drown their shores<br>As if the world were all dissolved to [[Tears\|tears]],<br>So high above his limits swells the rage<br>Of Bolingbroke, covering your fearful land<br>With hard bright steel, and hearts harder than steel.<br>Whitebeards have armed their thin and hairless scalps<br>Against thy majesty. Boys with women’s voices<br>Strive to speak big, and clap their female joints no<br>In stiff unwieldy arms against thy [[Crown\|crown]].<br>Thy very beadsmen learn to bend their bows<br>Of [[Double\|double]]-fatal yew against thy state.<br>Yea, distaff-women manage rusty bills<br>Against thy seat. Both young and old [[Rebel\|rebel]],<br>And all goes worse than I have power to tell. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Too [[Well\|well]], too [[Well\|well]] thou tell’st a tale so ill.<br>Where is the Earl of Wiltshire? Where is Bagot?<br>What is become of Bushy, where is Green,<br>That they have let the dangerous enemy<br>Measure our confines with such peaceful steps?<br>If we prevail, their heads shall pay for it.<br>I warrant they have made peace with Bolingbroke. |
| SCROPE | Peace have they made with him indeed, my lord. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | O villains, vipers damned without redemption!<br>[[dogs\|Dogs]] easily won to fawn on any man I<br>Snakes in my heart-[[Blood\|blood]] warmed, that sting my heart!<br>Three Judases, each one thrice-worse than Judas<br>Would they make peace? Terrible hell make war<br>Upon their spotted souls for this offence! |
| SCROPE | Sweet [[Love\|love]], I see, changing his property,<br>Turns to the sourest and most deadly hate.<br>Again uncurse their souls. Their peace is made<br>With heads, and not with hands. Those whom you<br>curse<br>Have felt the worst of [[Death\|death]]’s destroying wound,<br>And lie full low, graved in the hollow ground. |
| AUMERLE | Is Bushy, Green, and the Earl of Wiltshire dead? |
| SCROPE | Ay, all of them at Bristol lost their heads. |
| AUMERLE | Where is the Duke my father, with his power? |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | No matter where. Of comfort no man speak.<br>Let’s talk of graves, of worms and epitaphs,<br>Make [[Dust\|dust]] our paper, and with rainy eyes<br>Write sorrow on the bosom of the earth.<br>Let’s choose executors and talk of wills—<br>And yet not so, for what can we bequeath<br>Save our deposed bodies to the ground?<br>Our lands, our lives, and all are Bolingbroke’s;<br>And nothing can we call our own but [[Death\|death]],<br>And that small model of the barren earth<br>Which serves as paste and cover to our bones. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sitting For God’s sake, let us sit upon the ground, |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | And tell sad stories of the [[Death\|death]] of kings—<br>How some have been deposed, some slain in war,<br>Some haunted by the [[Ghosts\|ghosts]] they have deposed,<br>Some poisoned by their wives, some sleeping killed,<br>All murdered. For within the hollow [[Crown\|crown]]<br>That rounds the mortal temples of a [[King\|king]]<br>Keeps [[Death]] his [[Court\|court]]; and there the antic sits,<br>Scoffing his state and grinning at his pomp,<br>Allowing him a breath, a little scene,<br>To monarchize, be feared, and kill with looks,<br>Infusing him with [[Self\|self]] and vain conceit,<br>As if this flesh which walls about our life<br>Were brass impregnable; and humoured thus,<br>Comes at the last, and with a little pin<br>Bores through his [[Castle\|castle]] wall; and farewell, [[King\|king]].<br>Cover your heads, and mock not flesh and [[Blood\|blood]]<br>With solemn reverence. Throw away respect,<br>Tradition, form, and ceremonious duty,<br>For you have but mistook me all this while.<br>I live with bread, like you; feel want,<br>Taste grief, need friends. Subjected thus,<br>How can you say to me I am a [[King\|king]]? |
| BISHOP OF CARLISLE | My lord, wise men ne’er wail their present woes,<br>But presently prevent the ways to wail.<br>To [[Fear\|fear]] the foe, since [[Fear\|fear]] oppresseth strength,<br>Gives in your weakness strength unto your foe;<br>And so your follies fight against yourself.<br>[[Fear]], and be slain. No worse can come to fight;<br>And fight and die is [[Death\|death]] destroying [[Death\|death]],<br>Where fearing dying pays [[Death\|death]] servile breath. |
| AUMERLE | My father hath a power. Enquire of him,<br>And learn to make a [[Body\|body]] of a limb. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] standing |
| AUMERLE | Thou chid’st me [[Well\|well]]. Proud Bolingbroke, I come<br>To change blows with thee for our day of doom.<br>This ague-fit of [[Fear\|fear]] is overblown.<br>An easy task it is to win our own.<br>Say, Scrope, where lies our uncle with his power?<br>Speak sweetly, man, although thy looks be sour. |
| SCROPE | Men judge by the complexion of the sky<br>The state and inclination of the day.<br>So may you by my dull and heavy eye<br>My tongue hath but a heavier tale to say.<br>I play the torturer by small and small<br>To lengthen out the worst that must be spoken.<br>Your uncle York is joined with Bolingbroke,<br>And all your northern castles yielded up,<br>And all your southern gentlemen in arms<br>Upon his faction. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Thou hast said enough.<br>(To Aumerle) Beshrew thee, cousin, which didst lead me forth<br>Of that sweet way I was in to [[Despair\|despair]].<br>What say you now? What comfort have we now?<br>By heaven, I’ll hate him everlastingly<br>That bids me be of comfort any more.<br>Go to Flint [[Castle]]; there I’ll pine away.<br>A [[King\|king]], woe’s slave, shall kingly woe obey.<br>That power I have, discharge, and let them go<br>To ear the land that hath some [[Hope\|hope]] to grow;<br>For I have none. Let no man speak again<br>To alter this, for counsel is but vain. |
| AUMERLE | My liege, one word. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | He does me [[Double\|double]] wrong<br>That wounds me with the flatteries of his tongue.<br>Discharge my followers. Let them hence away<br>From [[Richard]]’s [[Night\|night]] to Bolingbroke’s fair day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bolingbroke Duke of Lancaster and Hereford, the [[Duke of York]], the [[Earl of Northumberland]], and soldiers, with drum and colours |
| BOLINGBROKE | So that by this intelligence we learn<br>The Welshmen are dispersed, and Salisbury<br>Is gone to meet the [[King]], who lately landed<br>With some few private friends upon this coast. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | The news is very fair and good, my lord.<br>[[Richard]] not far from hence hath hid his head. |
| YORK | It would beseem the Lord Northumberland<br>To say ‘[[King]] [[Richard]]’. Alack the heavy day<br>When such a sacred [[King\|king]] should hide his head! |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Your grace mistakes. Only to be brief<br>Left I his title out. |
| YORK | The [[Time\|time]] hath been,<br>Would you have been so brief with him, he would<br>Have been so brief with you to shorten you,<br>For taking so the head, your whole head’s length. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Mistake not, uncle, further than you should. |
| YORK | Take not, good cousin, further than you should,<br>Lest you mistake the heavens are over our heads. |
| BOLINGBROKE | I know it, uncle, and oppose not myself<br>Against their will. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Harry Percy and a trumpeter |
| BOLINGBROKE | But who comes here?<br>Welcome, Harry. What, will not this [[Castle\|castle]] yield? |
| HARRY PERCY | The [[Castle\|castle]] royally is manned, my lord,<br>Against thy entrance. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Royally?<br>Why, it contains no [[King\|king]]. |
| HARRY PERCY | Yes, my good lord,<br>It doth contain a [[King\|king]]. [[King]] [[Richard]] lies |
| *(didascalia)* | Within the limits of yon lime and stone, |
| HARRY PERCY | And with him are the Lord Aumerle, Lord Salisbury,<br>Sir Stephen Scrope, besides a clergyman<br>Of holy reverence; who, I cannot learn. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | O, belike it is the Bishop of Carlisle. |
| BOLINGBROKE | (to Northumberland) Noble lord,<br>Go to the rude ribs of that ancient [[Castle\|castle]];<br>Through brazen trumpet send the breath of parley<br>Into his ruined ears, and thus deliver.<br>[[Henry Bolingbroke]]<br>Upon his knees doth kiss [[King]] [[Richard]]’s hand,<br>And sends allegiance and true [[Faith\|faith]] of heart<br>To his most royal person, hither come<br>Even at his feet to lay my arms and power,<br>Provided that my banishment repealed<br>And lands restored again be freely granted.<br>If not, I’ll use the advantage of my power,<br>And lay the [[Summer\|summer]]’s [[Dust\|dust]] with showers of [[Blood\|blood]]<br>Rained from the wounds of slaughtered Englishmen;<br>The which how far off from the mind of Bolingbroke<br>It is such crimson tempest should bedrench<br>The fresh green lap of fair [[King]] [[Richard]]’s land,<br>My stooping duty tenderly shall show.<br>Go, signify as much, while here we march<br>Upon the grassy carpet of this plain.<br>Let’s march without the noise of threat‘ning drum,<br>That from this [[Castle\|castle]]’s tottered battlements<br>Our fair appointments may be [[Well\|well]] perused.<br>Methinks [[King]] [[Richard]] and myself should meet<br>With no less terror than the elements<br>Of [[Fire\|fire]] and [[Water\|water]] when their thund’ring shock<br>At meeting [[Tears\|tears]] the cloudy cheeks of heaven.<br>Be he the [[Fire\|fire]], I’ll be the yielding [[Water\|water]].<br>The rage be his, whilst on the earth I rain<br>My waters: on the earth, and not on him.—<br>March on, and mark [[King]] [[Richard]], how he looks. |
| *(didascalia)* | They march about the stage; then Bolingbroke, York, Percy, and soldiers stand at a distance from the walls; Northumberland and trumpeter advance to the walls. The trumpets sound Fa parley without, and an answer within; then a flourish within. [[King]] [[Richard]] appeareth on the walls, with the Bishop of Carlisle, the [[Duke of Aumerle]], Scrope, and the [[Earl of Salisbury]] |
| BOLINGBROKE | See, see, [[King]] [[Richard]] doth himself appear,<br>As doth the blushing discontented sun<br>From out the fiery portal of the east<br>When he perceives the envious clouds are bent<br>To dim his glory and to stain the track<br>Of his bright passage to the occident. |
| YORK | Yet looks he like a [[King\|king]]. Behold, his eye,<br>As bright as is the eagle’s, lightens forth<br>Controlling majesty. Alack, alack for woe<br>That any harm should stain so fair a show! |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | (to Northumberland)<br>We are amazed; and thus long have we stood<br>To watch the fearful bending of thy knee,<br>Because we thought ourself thy lawful [[King\|king]].<br>An if we be, how dare thy joints forget<br>To pay their aweful duty to our presence?<br>If we be not, show us the hand of God<br>That hath dismissed us from our stewardship.<br>For [[Well\|well]] we know no hand of [[Blood\|blood]] and bone<br>Can grip the sacred handle of our sceptre,<br>Unless he do profane, steal, or usurp.<br>And though you think that all—as you have done—<br>Have torn their souls by turning them from us,<br>And we are barren and bereft of friends,<br>Yet know my master, God omnipotent,<br>Is mustering in his clouds on our behalf<br>Armies of pestilence; and they shall strike<br>Your children yet [[unborn]] and unbegot,<br>That lift your vassal hands against my head<br>And threat the glory of my precious [[Crown\|crown]].<br>Tell Bolingbroke, for yon methinks he is,<br>That every stride he makes upon my land<br>Is dangerous treason. He is come to open<br>The purple testament of bleeding war;<br>But ere the [[Crown\|crown]] he looks for live in peace<br>Ten thousand bloody crowns of mothers’ sons<br>Shall ill become the flower of England’s face,<br>Change the complexion of her maid-pale peace<br>To scarlet indignation, and bedew<br>Her pastures’ grass with faithful English [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | NORTHUMBERLAND kneeling |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | The [[King]] of heaven forbid our lord the [[King]]<br>Should so with civil and uncivil arms<br>Be rushed upon. Thy thrice-noble cousin<br>Harry Bolingbroke doth humbly kiss thy hand,<br>And by the honourable tomb he swears,<br>That stands upon your royal grandsire’s bones,<br>And by the royalties of both your bloods,<br>Currents that [[Spring\|spring]] from one most gracious head,<br>And by the buried hand of warlike Gaunt,<br>And by the worth and [[Honour\|honour]] of himself,<br>Comprising all that may be sworn or said, no<br>His coming hither hath no further scope<br>Than for his lineal royalties, and to beg<br>Enfranchisement immediate on his knees;<br>Which on thy royal party granted once,<br>His glittering arms he will commend to rust,<br>His barbed steeds to stables, and his heart<br>To faithful service of your majesty.<br>This swears he as he is a prince and just,<br>And as I am a gentleman I credit him. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Northumberland, say thus the [[King]] returns:<br>His noble cousin is right welcome hither,<br>And all the number of his fair demands<br>Shall be accomplished without contradiction.<br>With all the gracious utterance thou hast,<br>Speak to his gentle hearing kind commends.<br>Northumberland and the trumpeter return to Bolingbroke<br>(To Aumerle) We do debase ourself, cousin, do we not,<br>To look so poorly and to speak so fair?<br>Shall we call back Northumberland, and send<br>Defiance to the traitor, and so die? |
| AUMERLE | No, good my lord, let’s fight with gentle words<br>Till [[Time\|time]] lend friends, and friends their helpful swords. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | O God, O God, that e‘er this tongue of mine,<br>That laid the sentence of dread banishment<br>On yon proud man, should take it off again<br>With words of sooth! O, that I were as great<br>As is my grief, or lesser than my name,<br>Or that I could forget what I have been,<br>Or not remember what I must be now!<br>Swell’st thou, proud heart? I’ll give thee scope to beat,<br>Since foes have scope to beat both thee and me.<br>Northumberland advances to the walls |
| AUMERLE | Northumberland comes back from Bolingbroke. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | What must the [[King]] do now? Must he submit?<br>The [[King]] shall do it. Must he be deposed?<br>The [[King]] shall be contented. Must he lose<br>The name of [[King]]? A God’s name, let it go.<br>I’ll give my jewels for a set of beads,<br>My gorgeous palace for a hermitage,<br>My gay apparel for an almsman’s gown,<br>My figured goblets for a dish of wood,<br>My sceptre for a palmer’s walking staff,<br>My subjects for a pair of carved saints,<br>And my large kingdom for a little [[Grave\|grave]],<br>A little, little [[Grave\|grave]], an obscure [[Grave\|grave]];<br>Or I’ll be buried in the [[King]]’s highway,<br>Some way of common trade where subjects’ feet<br>May hourly trample on their sovereign’s head,<br>For on my heart they tread now, whilst I live,<br>And buried once, why not upon my head?<br>Aumerle, thou weep‘st, my tender-hearted cousin.<br>We’ll make foul weather with despised [[Tears\|tears]].<br>Our sighs and they shall lodge the [[Summer\|summer]] corn,<br>And make a dearth in this revolting land.<br>Or shall we play the wantons with our woes,<br>And make some pretty match with shedding [[Tears\|tears]];<br>As thus to drop them still upon one place<br>Till they have fretted us a pair of graves |
| *(didascalia)* | Within the earth, and therein laid? ‘There lies |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Two kinsmen digged their graves with weeping eyes.’<br>Would not this ill do [[Well\|well]]? [[Well]], [[Well\|well]], I see<br>I talk but idly and you mock at me.<br>Most mighty prince, my lord Northumberland,<br>What says [[King]] Bolingbroke? Will his majesty<br>Give [[Richard]] leave to live till [[Richard]] die?<br>You make a leg, and Bolingbroke says ‘Ay’. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | My lord, in the base [[Court\|court]] he doth attend<br>To speak with you. May it please you to come down? |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Down, down I come like glist’ring Phaethon,<br>Wanting the manage of unruly jades.<br>In the base [[Court\|court]]: base [[Court\|court]] where kings grow base<br>To come at traitors’ calls, and do them grace.<br>In the base [[Court\|court]], come down: down [[Court\|court]], down<br>[[King]],<br>For [[Night\|night]]-owls shriek where mounting larks should sing. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[King]] [[Richard]] and his party |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Northumberland returns to Bolingbroke |
| BOLINGBROKE | What says his majesty? |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Sorrow and grief of heart<br>Makes him speak fondly, like a frantic man. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] [[Richard]] and his party below |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Yet he is come. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Stand all apart,<br>And show fair duty to his majesty.<br>He kneels down<br>My gracious lord. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Fair cousin, you debase your princely knee<br>To make the base earth proud with kissing it.<br>Me rather had my heart might feel your [[Love\|love]]<br>Than my unpleased eye see your courtesy.<br>Up, cousin, up. Your heart is up, I know,<br>Thus high at least, although your knee be low. |
| BOLINGBROKE | My gracious lord, I come but for mine own. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Your own is yours, and I am yours, and all. |
| BOLINGBROKE | So far be mine, my most redoubted lord,<br>As my true service shall deserve your [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | [[Well]] you deserve. They [[Well\|well]] deserve to have<br>That know the strong’st and surest way to get. |
| *(didascalia)* | Bolingbroke rises |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | (To York) Uncle, give me your hands. Nay, dry your<br>eyes.<br>[[Tears]] show their [[Love\|love]], but want their remedies.<br>(To Bolingbroke) Cousin, I am too young to be your father,<br>Though you are old enough to be my heir.<br>What you will have I’ll give, and willing too;<br>For do we must what force will have us do.<br>Set on towards [[London]], cousin: is it so? |
| BOLINGBROKE | Yea, my good lord. |
| [[King\|KING]] [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Then I must not say no. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Queen]], with her two Ladies |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | What sport shall we devise here in this [[Garden\|garden]],<br>To drive away the heavy thought of care? |
| *(didascalia)* | first LADY Madam, we’ll play at bowls. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | ’Twill make me think the world is full of rubs,<br>And that my fortune runs against the bias. |
| *(didascalia)* | SECOND LADY Madam, we’ll [[Dance\|dance]]. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | My legs can keep no measure in delight<br>When my poor heart no measure keeps in grief;<br>Therefore no dancing, girl. Some other sport. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST LADY Madam, we’ll tell tales. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Of sorrow or of joy? |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST LADY Of either, madam. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Of neither, girl.<br>For if of joy, being altogether wanting,<br>It doth remember me the more of sorrow.<br>Or if of grief, being altogether had,<br>It adds more sorrow to my want of joy.<br>For what I have I need not to repeat,<br>And what I want it boots not to complain. |
| *(didascalia)* | SECOND LADY |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Madam, I’ll sing. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | ’Tis [[Well\|well]] that thou hast cause;<br>But thou shouldst please me better wouldst thou weep. |
| *(didascalia)* | SECOND LADY |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | I could weep, madam, would it do you good. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | And I could sing, would weeping do me good,<br>And never borrow any tear of thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Gardener and two Men |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | But stay; here come the gardeners.<br>Let’s step into the [[Shadow\|shadow]] of these trees.<br>My wretchedness unto a row of pins<br>They will talk of state, for everyone doth so<br>Against a change. Woe is forerun with woe.<br>The [[Queen]] and her Ladies stand apart |
| *(didascalia)* | GARDENER to First Man |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Go, bind thou up young dangling apricots<br>Which, like unruly children, make their sire<br>Stoop with oppression of their prodigal weight.<br>Give some supportance to the bending twigs. |
| *(didascalia)* | To Second Man Go thou, and, like an executioner, |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Cut off the heads of too fast-growing sprays<br>That look too lofty in our commonwealth.<br>All must be even in our [[government]].<br>You thus employed, I will go root away<br>The noisome weeds which without profit suck<br>The soil’s fertility from wholesome [[Flowers\|flowers]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST MAN |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Why should we, in the compass of a pale,<br>Keep law and form and due proportion,<br>Showing as in a model our firm estate,<br>When our sea-wallèd [[Garden\|garden]], the whole land,<br>Is full of weeds, her fairest [[Flowers\|flowers]] choked up,<br>Her fruit trees all unpruned, her hedges ruined,<br>Her knots disordered, and her wholesome herbs<br>Swarming with caterpillars? |
| GARDENER | Hold thy peace.<br>He that hath suffered this disordered [[Spring\|spring]]<br>Hath now himself met with the fall of leaf.<br>The weeds which his broad spreading [[Leaves\|leaves]] did<br>shelter,<br>That seemed in eating him to hold him up,<br>Are plucked up, root and all, by Bolingbroke—<br>I mean the Earl of Wiltshire, Bushy, Green. |
| *(didascalia)* | SECOND MAN |
| GARDENER | What, are they dead? |
| GARDENER | They are; and Bolingbroke<br>Hath seized the wasteful [[King]]. O, what pity is it<br>That he had not so trimmed and dressed his land<br>As we this [[Garden\|garden]]! We at [[Time\|time]] of year<br>Do wound the bark, the skin of our fruit trees,<br>Lest, being over-proud in sap and [[Blood\|blood]],<br>With too much riches it confound itself.<br>Had he done so to great and growing men,<br>They might have lived to bear, and he to taste,<br>Their fruits of duty. Superfluous branches<br>We lop away, that bearing boughs may live.<br>Had he done so, himself had borne the [[Crown\|crown]],<br>Which waste of idle hours hath quite thrown down. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST MAN |
| GARDENER | What, think you then the [[King]] shall be deposed? |
| GARDENER | Depressed he is already, and deposed<br>’Tis [[Doubt\|doubt]] he will be. Letters came last [[Night\|night]]<br>To a dear friend of the good [[Duke of York]]’s<br>That tell black tidings. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | O, I am pressed to [[Death\|death]] through want of speaking!<br>She comes forward<br>Thou, old Adam’s likeness, set to dress this [[Garden\|garden]],<br>How dares thy harsh rude tongue sound this unpleasing news?<br>What Eve, what serpent hath suggested thee<br>To make a second fall of cursed man?<br>Why dost thou say [[King]] [[Richard]] is deposed?<br>Dar‘st thou, thou little better thing than earth,<br>Divine his downfall? Say where, when, and how<br>Cam’st thou by this ill tidings? Speak, thou wretch! |
| GARDENER | Pardon me, madam. Little joy have I<br>To breathe this news, yet what I say is true.<br>[[King]] [[Richard]] he is in the mighty hold<br>Of Bolingbroke. Their fortunes both are weighed.<br>In your lord’s scale is nothing but himself<br>And some few vanities that make him light.<br>But in the balance of great Bolingbroke,<br>Besides himself, are all the English peers,<br>And with that odds he weighs [[King]] [[Richard]] down.<br>Post you to [[London]] and you will find it so.<br>I speak no more than everyone doth know. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Nimble mischance that art so light of foot,<br>Doth not thy embassage belong to me,<br>And am I last that knows it? O, thou think‘st<br>To serve me last, that I may longest keep<br>Thy sorrow in my breast. Come, ladies, go<br>To meet at [[London]] [[London]]’s [[King\|king]] in woe.<br>What, was I born to this, that my sad look<br>Should grace the triumph of great Bolingbroke?<br>Gard’ner, for telling me these news of woe,<br>Pray God the plants thou graft’st may never grow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with her Ladies |
| GARDENER | Poor [[Queen]], so that thy state might be no worse<br>I would my skill were subject to thy curse.<br>Here did she fall a tear. Here in this place<br>I’ll set a bank of rue, sour herb-of-grace.<br>Rue even for ruth here shortly shall be seen<br>In the remembrance of a weeping [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter, as to Parliament, Bolingbroke Duke of Lancaster and Hereford, the [[Duke of Aumerle]], the [[Earl of Northumberland]], Harry Percy, Lord Fitzwalter, the Duke of Surrey, the Bishop of Carlisle, and the Abbot of Westminster |
| BOLINGBROKE | Call forth Bagot. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bagot, with officers |
| BOLINGBROKE | Now, Bagot, freely speak thy mind:<br>What thou dost know of noble [[Gloucester]]’s [[Death\|death]],<br>Who wrought it with the [[King]], and who performed<br>The bloody office of his timeless end. |
| BAGOT | Then set before my face the Lord Aumerle. |
| BOLINGBROKE | (to Aumerle)<br>Cousin, stand forth, and look upon that man.<br>Aumerle stands forth |
| BAGOT | My lord Aumerle, I know your daring tongue<br>Scorns to unsay what once it hath delivered.<br>In that dead [[Time\|time]] when [[Gloucester]]’s [[Death\|death]] was plotted<br>I heard you say ‘Is not my arm of length,<br>That reacheth from the restful English [[Court\|court]]<br>As far as Calais, to mine uncle’s head?’<br>Amongst much other talk that very [[Time\|time]]<br>I heard you say that you had rather refuse<br>The offer of an hundred thousand crowns<br>Than Bolingbroke’s return to England,<br>Adding withal how blest this land would be<br>In this your cousin’s [[Death\|death]]. |
| AUMERLE | Princes and noble lords,<br>What answer shall I make to this base man?<br>Shall I so much dishonour my fair [[Stars\|stars]]<br>On equal terms to give him chastisement?<br>Either I must, or have mine [[Honour\|honour]] soiled<br>With the attainder of his slanderous lips.<br>He throws down his gage<br>There is my gage, the manual seal of [[Death\|death]]<br>That marks thee out for hell. I say thou liest,<br>And will maintain what thou hast said is false<br>In thy heart [[Blood\|blood]], though being all too base<br>To stain the temper of my knightly [[Sword\|sword]]. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Bagot, forbear. Thou shalt not take it up. |
| AUMERLE | Excepting one, I would he were the best<br>In all this presence that hath moved me so. |
| FITZWALTER | If that thy valour stand on sympathy,<br>There is my gage, Aumerle, in gage to thine.<br>He throws down his gage<br>By that fair sun which shows me where thou stand‘st,<br>I heard thee say, and vauntingly thou spak’st it,<br>That thou wert cause of noble [[Gloucester]]’s [[Death\|death]].<br>If thou deny’st it twenty times, thou liest,<br>And I will turn thy falsehood to thy heart,<br>Where it was forged, with my rapier’s point. |
| AUMERLE | Thou dar’st not, coward, live to see that day. |
| FITZWALTER | Now by my [[Soul\|soul]], I would it were this hour. |
| AUMERLE | Fitzwalter, thou art damned to hell for this. |
| HARRY PERCY | Aumerle, thou liest. His [[Honour\|honour]] is as true<br>In this appeal as thou art all unjust;<br>And that thou art so, there I throw my gage<br>He throws down his gage<br>To prove it on thee to the extremest point<br>Of mortal breathing. Seize it if thou dar’st. |
| AUMERLE | An if I do not, may my hands rot off,<br>And never brandish more revengeful steel<br>Over the glittering helmet of my foe. |
| SURREY | My lord Fitzwalter, I do remember [[Well\|well]]<br>The very [[Time\|time]] Aumerle and you did talk. |
| FITZWALTER | ’Tis very true. You were in presence then,<br>And you can witness with me this is true. |
| SURREY | As false, by heaven, as heaven itself is true. |
| FITZWALTER | Surrey, thou liest. |
| SURREY | Dishonourable boy,<br>That lie shall lie so heavy on my [[Sword\|sword]]<br>That it shall render vengeance and [[Revenge\|revenge]],<br>Till thou, the lie-giver, and that lie do lie<br>In earth as quiet as thy father’s skull;<br>In proof whereof, there is my [[Honour\|honour]]’s pawn.<br>He throws down his gage<br>Engage it to the trial if thou dar’st. |
| FITZWALTER | How fondly dost thou spur a forward [[Horse\|horse]]!<br>If I dare eat, or drink, or breathe, or live,<br>I dare meet Surrey in a wilderness<br>And spit upon him whilst I say he lies,<br>And lies, and lies. There is my bond of [[Faith\|faith]]<br>To tie thee to my strong correction.<br>As I intend to thrive in this new world,<br>Aumerle is guilty of my true appeal.<br>Besides, I heard the banished Norfolk say<br>That thou, Aumerle, didst send two of thy men<br>To execute the noble Duke at Calais. |
| AUMERLE | Some honest Christian trust me with a gage.<br>He takes another’s gage and throws it down<br>That Norfolk lies, here do I throw down this,<br>If he may be repealed, to try his [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| BOLINGBROKE | These differences shall all rest under gage<br>Till Norfolk be repealed. Repealed he shall be,<br>And, though mine enemy, restored again<br>To all his lands and signories. When he is returned,<br>Against Aumerle we will enforce his trial. |
| BISHOP OF CARLISLE | That honourable day shall never be seen.<br>Many a [[Time\|time]] hath banished Norfolk fought<br>For Jesu [[Christ]] in glorious Christian field,<br>Streaming the ensign of the Christian [[Cross\|cross]]<br>Against black pagans, Turks, and Saracens;<br>And, toiled with works of war, retired himself<br>To [[Italy]], and there at [[Venice]] gave<br>His [[Body\|body]] to that pleasant country’s earth,<br>And his pure [[Soul\|soul]] unto his captain, [[Christ]],<br>Under whose colours he had fought so long. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Why, Bishop of Carlisle, is Norfolk dead? |
| BISHOP OF CARLISLE | As surely as I live, my lord. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Sweet peace conduct his sweet [[Soul\|soul]] to the bosom<br>Of good old Abraham! Lords appellants,<br>Your differences shall all rest under gage<br>Till we assign you to your days of trial. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Duke of York]] |
| YORK | Great Duke of Lancaster, I come to thee<br>From plume-plucked [[Richard]], who with willing [[Soul\|soul]]<br>Adopts thee heir, and his high sceptre yields<br>To the possession of thy royal hand.<br>Ascend his throne, descending now from him,<br>And long live Henry, of that name the fourth! |
| BOLINGBROKE | In God’s name I’ll ascend the regal throne. |
| BISHOP OF CARLISLE | Marry, God forbid!<br>Worst in this royal presence may I speak,<br>Yet best beseeming me to speak the [[Truth\|truth]].<br>Would God that any in this noble presence<br>Were enough noble to be upright judge<br>Of noble [[Richard]]. Then true noblesse would<br>Learn him forbearance from so foul a wrong.<br>What subject can give sentence on his [[King\|king]]?<br>And who sits here that is not [[Richard]]’s subject?<br>Thieves are not judged but they are by to hear,<br>Although apparent [[Guilt\|guilt]] be seen in them;<br>And shall the figure of God’s majesty,<br>His captain, steward, deputy elect,<br>Anointed, crowned, planted many years,<br>Be judged by subject and inferior breath,<br>And he himself not present? O, forfend it, God,<br>That in a Christian climate souls refined<br>Should show so heinous, black, obscene a deed!<br>I speak to subjects, and a subject speaks<br>Stirred up by God thus boldly for his [[King\|king]].<br>My lord of Hereford here, whom you call [[King\|king]],<br>Is a foul traitor to proud Hereford’s [[King\|king]];<br>And, if you [[Crown\|crown]] him, let me prophesy<br>The [[Blood\|blood]] of English shall manure the ground,<br>And future ages groan for this foul act.<br>Peace shall go [[Sleep\|sleep]] with Turks and infidels,<br>And in this seat of peace tumultuous wars<br>Shall kin with kin and kind with kind confound.<br>Disorder, horror, [[Fear\|fear]], and mutiny<br>Shall here inhabit, and this land be called<br>The field of Golgotha and dead men’s skulls.<br>O, if you rear this [[house]] against this [[house]]<br>It will the woefullest division prove<br>That ever fell upon this cursed earth!<br>Prevent, resist it; let it not be so,<br>Lest [[Child\|child]], [[Child\|child]]’s children, cry against you woe. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | [[Well]] have you argued, sir, and for your pains<br>Of capital treason we arrest you here.<br>My lord of Westminster, be it your charge<br>To keep him safely till his day of trial.<br>May it please you, lords, to grant the Commons’ suit? |
| BOLINGBROKE | Fetch hither [[Richard]], that in common view<br>He may surrender. So we shall proceed<br>Without suspicion. |
| YORK | I will be his conduct. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| BOLINGBROKE | Lords, you that here are under our arrest,<br>Procure your sureties for your days of answer.<br>Little are we beholden to your [[Love\|love]],<br>And little looked for at your helping hands. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Richard]] and the [[Duke of York]], with attendants bearing the [[Crown\|crown]] and sceptre |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Alack, why am I sent for to a [[King\|king]]<br>Before I have shook off the regal thoughts<br>Wherewith I reigned? I hardly yet have learned<br>To insinuate, flatter, bow, and bend my knee.<br>Give sorrow leave awhile to tutor me<br>To this submission. Yet I [[Well\|well]] remember<br>The favours of these men. Were they not mine?<br>Did they not sometime cry ‘All haill’ to me?<br>So Judas did to [[Christ]]. But He in twelve<br>Found [[Truth\|truth]] in all but one; I, in twelve thousand, none.<br>God save the [[King]] ! Will no man say ‘Amen’ ?<br>Am I both priest and clerk? [[Well]] then, Amen.<br>God save the [[King]], although I be not he.<br>And yet Amen, if heaven do think him me.<br>To do what service am I sent for hither? |
| YORK | To do that office of thine own good will<br>Which tired majesty did make thee offer:<br>The [[Resignation\|resignation]] of thy state and [[Crown\|crown]]<br>To [[Henry Bolingbroke]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | (to an attendant)<br>Give me the [[Crown\|crown]]. (To Bolingbroke) Here, cousin, seize the [[Crown\|crown]].<br>Here, cousin. On this side my hand, on that side thine.<br>Now is this golden [[Crown\|crown]] like a deep [[Well\|well]]<br>That owes two buckets filling one another,<br>The emptier ever dancing in the air,<br>The other down, unseen, and full of [[Water\|water]].<br>That bucket down and full of [[Tears\|tears]] am I,<br>Drinking my griefs, whilst you mount up on high. |
| BOLINGBROKE | I thought you had been willing to resign. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | My [[Crown\|crown]] I am, but still my griefs are mine.<br>You may my glories and my state depose,<br>But not my griefs; still am I [[King\|king]] of those. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Part of your cares you give me with your [[Crown\|crown]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Your cares set up do not pluck my cares down.<br>My care is loss of care by old care done;<br>Your care is gain of care by new care won.<br>The cares I give I have, though given away;<br>They ’tend the [[Crown\|crown]], yet still with me they stay. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Are you contented to resign the [[Crown\|crown]]? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Ay, no; no, ay; for I must nothing be;<br>Therefore no, no, for I resign to thee.<br>Now mark me how I will undo myself.<br>I give this heavy weight from off my head,<br>[Bolingbroke accepts the [[Crown\|crown]]]<br>And this unwieldy sceptre from my hand, |
| *(didascalia)* | Bolingbroke accepts the sceptre |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | The [[Pride\|pride]] of kingly sway from out my heart.<br>With mine own [[Tears\|tears]] I wash away my balm,<br>With mine own hands I give away my [[Crown\|crown]],<br>With mine own tongue deny my sacred state,<br>With mine own breath release all duteous oaths.<br>All pomp and majesty I do forswear.<br>My manors, rents, revenues I forgo.<br>My acts, decrees, and statutes I deny.<br>God pardon all oaths that are broke to me.<br>God keep all vows unbroke are made to thee.<br>Make me, that nothing have, with nothing grieved,<br>And thou with all pleased, that hast all achieved.<br>Long mayst thou live in [[Richard]]’s seat to sit,<br>And soon lie [[Richard]] in an earthy pit.<br>‘God save [[King]] Henry,’ unkinged [[Richard]] says,<br>‘And send him many years of sunshine days.’<br>What more remains? |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | (giving [[Richard]] papers)<br>No more but that you read<br>These accusations and these grievous crimes<br>Committed by your person and your followers<br>Against the state and profit of this land,<br>That by confessing them, the souls of men<br>May deem that you are worthily deposed. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Must I do so? And must I ravel out<br>My weaved-up follies ? Gentle Northumberland,<br>If thy offences were upon record,<br>Would it not shame thee in so fair a troop<br>To read a lecture of them? If thou wouldst,<br>There shouldst thou find one heinous article<br>Containing the deposing of a [[King\|king]]<br>And cracking the strong warrant of an oath,<br>Marked with a blot, damned in the book of heaven.<br>Nay, all of you that stand and look upon<br>Whilst that my wretchedness doth bait myself,<br>Though some of you, with Pilate, wash your hands,<br>Showing an outward pity, yet you Pitates<br>Have here delivered me to my sour [[Cross\|cross]],<br>And [[Water\|water]] cannot wash away your sin. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | My lord, dispatch. Read o’er these articles. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Mine eyes are full of [[Tears\|tears]]; I cannot see.<br>And yet salt [[Water\|water]] blinds them not so much<br>But they can see a sort of traitors here.<br>Nay, if I turn mine eyes upon myself<br>I find myself a traitor with the rest,<br>For I have given here my [[Soul\|soul]]’s consent<br>T’undeck the pompous [[Body\|body]] of a [[King\|king]],<br>Made glory base and sovereignty a slave,<br>Proud majesty a subject, state a peasant. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | My lord— |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | No lord of thine, thou haught-insulting man,<br>Nor no man’s lord. I have no name, no title,<br>No, not that name was given me at the font,<br>But ’tis usurped. Alack the heavy day,<br>That I have worn so many winters out<br>And know not now what name to call myself!<br>O, that I were a mockery [[King\|king]] of [[Snow\|snow]],<br>Standing before the sun of Bolingbroke<br>To melt myself away in [[Water\|water]]-drops !<br>Good [[King\|king]], great [[King\|king]]—and yet not greatly good—<br>An if my word be sterling yet in England,<br>Let it command a [[Mirror\|mirror]] hither straight,<br>That it may show me what a face I have,<br>Since it is bankrupt of his majesty. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Go some of you and fetch a looking-glass. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one or more |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Read o’er this paper while the glass doth come. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Fiend, thou torment’st me ere I come to hell. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Urge it no more, my lord Northumberland. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | The Commons will not then be satisfied. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | They shall be satisfied. I’ll read enough<br>When I do see the very book indeed<br>Where all my sins are writ, and that’s myself. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter one with a glass |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Give me that glass, and therein will I read.<br>[[Richard]] takes the glass and looks in it<br>No deeper wrinkles yet? Hath sorrow struck<br>So many blows upon this face of mine<br>And made no deeper wounds? O flatt’ring glass,<br>Like to my followers in prosperity,<br>Thou dost beguile me! Was this face the face<br>That every day under his household roof<br>Did keep ten thousand men? Was this the face<br>That like the sun did make beholders wink?<br>Is this the face which faced so many follies,<br>That was at last outfaced by Bolingbroke?<br>A brittle glory shineth in this face.<br>As brittle as the glory is the face,<br>He shatters the glass<br>For there it is, cracked in an hundred shivers.<br>Mark, silent [[King]], the moral of this sport:<br>How soon my sorrow hath destroyed my face. |
| BOLINGBROKE | The [[Shadow\|shadow]] of your sorrow hath destroyed<br>The [[Shadow\|shadow]] of your face. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Say that again:<br>‘The [[Shadow\|shadow]] of my sorrow’—ha, let’s see.<br>‘Tis very true: my grief lies all within,<br>And these external manner of laments<br>Are merely shadows to the unseen grief<br>That swells with [[Silence\|silence]] in the tortured [[Soul\|soul]].<br>There lies the substance, and I thank thee, [[King]],<br>For thy great bounty that not only giv’st<br>Me cause to wail, but teachest me the way<br>How to lament the cause. I’ll beg one boon,<br>And then be gone and trouble you no more.<br>Shall I obtain it? |
| BOLINGBROKE | Name it, fair cousin. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Fair cousin? I am greater than a [[King\|king]];<br>For when I was a [[King\|king]] my flatterers<br>Were then but subjects; being now a subject,<br>I have a [[King\|king]] here to my flatterer.<br>Being so great, I have no need to beg. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Yet ask. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | And shall I have? |
| BOLINGBROKE | You shall. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Then give me leave to go. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Whither? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Whither you will, so I were from your sights. |
| BOLINGBROKE | Go some of you, convey him to the Tower. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | O good, ‘convey’! Conveyors are you all,<br>That rise thus nimbly by a true [[King\|king]]’s fall. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit, guarded |
| BOLINGBROKE | On Wednesday next we solemnly set down<br>Our coronation. Lords, prepare yourselves. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but the Abbot of Westminster, the Bishop of Carlisle, and Aumerle |
| ABBOT OF WESTMINSTER | A woeful pageant have we here beheld. |
| BISHOP OF CARLISLE | The woe’s to come, the children yet [[unborn]]<br>Shall feel this day as sharp to them as thorn. |
| AUMERLE | You holy clergymen, is there no plot<br>To rid the realm of this pernicious blot? |
| ABBOT OF WESTMINSTER | My lord, before I freely speak my mind herein,<br>You shall not only take the sacrament<br>To bury mine intents, but also to effect<br>Whatever I shall happen to devise.<br>I see your brows are full of discontent,<br>Your hearts of sorrow, and your eyes of [[Tears\|tears]].<br>Come [[Home\|home]] with me to supper. I will lay<br>A plot shall show us all a merry day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Queen]], with her Ladies |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | This way the [[King]] will come. This is the way<br>To [[Julius Caesar]]’s ill-erected Tower,<br>To whose flint bosom my condemned lord<br>Is doomed a prisoner by proud Bolingbroke.<br>Here let us rest, if this rebellious earth<br>Have any resting for her true [[King\|king]]’s [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Richard]] and guard |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | But soft, but see—or rather do not see—<br>My fair [[Rose\|rose]] wither. Yet look up, behold,<br>That you in pity may dissolve to dew,<br>And wash him fresh again with true-[[Love\|love]] [[Tears\|tears]].—<br>Ah, thou the model where old Troy did stand!<br>Thou map of [[Honour\|honour]], thou [[King]] [[Richard]]’s tomb,<br>And not [[King]] [[Richard]]! Thou most beauteous inn:<br>Why should hard-favoured grief be lodged in thee,<br>When triumph is become an alehouse guest? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Join not with grief, fair woman, do not so,<br>To make my end too sudden. Learn, good [[Soul\|soul]],<br>To think our former state a happy dream,<br>From which awaked, the [[Truth\|truth]] of what we are<br>Shows us but this. I am sworn brother, sweet,<br>To grim necessity, and he and I<br>Will keep a league till [[Death\|death]]. Hie thee to France,<br>And cloister thee in some religious [[house]].<br>Our holy lives must win a new world’s [[Crown\|crown]],<br>Which our profane hours here have stricken down. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | What, is my [[Richard]] both in shape and mind<br>Transformed and weakenèd? Hath Bolingbroke<br>Deposed thine intellect? Hath he been in thy heart?<br>The [[Lion\|lion]] dying thrusteth forth his paw<br>And wounds the earth, if nothing else, with rage<br>To be o’erpowered; and wilt thou, pupil-like,<br>Take the correction, mildly kiss the rod,<br>And fawn on rage with base [[Humility\|humility]],<br>Which art a [[Lion\|lion]] and the [[King\|king]] of beasts ? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | A [[King\|king]] of beasts indeed! If aught but beasts,<br>I had been still a happy [[King\|king]] of men.<br>Good sometimes [[Queen]], prepare thee hence for France.<br>Think I am dead, and that even here thou tak’st,<br>As from my [[Death\|death]]-bed, thy last living leave.<br>In [[Winter\|winter]]’s tedious nights, sit by the [[Fire\|fire]]<br>With good old folks, and let them tell thee tales<br>Of woeful ages long ago betid;<br>And ere thou bid goodnight, to quit their griefs<br>Tell thou the lamentable fall of me,<br>And send the hearers weeping to their beds;<br>Forwhy the senseless brands will sympathize<br>The heavy accent of thy moving tongue,<br>And in compassion weep the [[Fire\|fire]] out;<br>And some will mourn in ashes, some coal black,<br>For the deposing of a rightful [[King\|king]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Northumberland]] |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | My lord, the mind of Bolingbroke is changed.<br>You must to Pomfret, not unto the Tower.<br>And, madam, there is order ta’en for you.<br>With all swift [[Speed\|speed]] you must away to France. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Northumberland, thou ladder wherewithal<br>The mounting Bolingbroke ascends my throne,<br>The [[Time\|time]] shall not be many hours of age<br>More than it is ere foul sin, gathering head,<br>Shall break into corruption. Thou shalt think,<br>Though he divide the realm and give thee half,<br>It is too little helping him to all.<br>He shall think that thou, which know‘st the way<br>To plant unrightful kings, wilt know again,<br>Being ne’er so little urged another way,<br>To pluck him headlong from the usurped throne.<br>The [[Love\|love]] of wicked friends converts to [[Fear\|fear]],<br>That [[Fear\|fear]] to hate, and hate turns one or both<br>To worthy danger and deserved [[Death\|death]]. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | My [[Guilt\|guilt]] be on my head, and there an end.<br>Take leave and part, for you must part forthwith. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Doubly divorced! Bad men, you violate<br>A twofold [[Marriage\|marriage]]:‘twixt my [[Crown\|crown]] and me,<br>And then betwixt me and my married wife.<br>(To the [[Queen]]) Let me unkiss the oath ’twixt thee and me—<br>And yet not so, for with a kiss ‘twas made.<br>Part us, Northumberland: I towards the north,<br>Where shivering cold and sickness pines the clime;<br>My [[Queen\|queen]] to France, from whence set forth in pomp<br>She came adorned hither like sweet May,<br>Sent back like Hallowmas or short’st of day. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | And must we be divided? Must we part? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Ay, hand from hand, my [[Love\|love]], and heart from heart. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Banish us both, and send the [[King]] with me. |
| *(didascalia)* | NORTHUMBERLAND |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | That were some [[Love\|love]], but little policy. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Then whither he goes, thither let me go. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | So two together weeping make one woe.<br>Weep thou for me in France, I for thee here.<br>Better far off than, near, be ne’er the nea’er.<br>Go count thy way with sighs, I mine with groans. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | So longest way shall have the longest moans. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Twice for one step I’ll groan, the way being short,<br>And piece the way out with a heavy heart.<br>Come, come, in wooing sorrow let’s be brief,<br>Since, wedding it, there is such length in grief.<br>One kiss shall stop our mouths, and dumbly part.<br>Thus give I mine, and thus take I thy heart.<br>They kiss |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Give me mine own again. ’Twere no good part<br>To take on me to keep and kill thy heart.<br>They kiss<br>So now I have mine own again, be gone,<br>That I may strive to kill it with a groan. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | We make woe wanton with this fond delay.<br>Once more, adieu. The rest let sorrow say. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt FRichard, guarded, and Northumberland at one door, the [[Queen]] and her Ladies at another door |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Duke and Duchess of York |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | My lord, you told me you would tell the rest,<br>When weeping made you break the story off,<br>Of our two cousins’ coming into [[London]]. |
| YORK | Where did I leave? |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | At that sad stop, my lord,<br>Where rude misgoverned hands from windows’ tops<br>Threw [[Dust\|dust]] and rubbish on [[King]] [[Richard]]’s head. |
| YORK | Then, as I said, the Duke, great Bolingbroke,<br>Mounted upon a hot and fiery steed,<br>Which his aspiring rider seemed to know,<br>With slow but stately pace kept on his course,<br>Whilst all tongues cried ‘God save thee, Bolingbroke!’<br>You would have thought the very windows spake,<br>So many greedy looks of young and old<br>Through casements darted their desiring eyes<br>Upon his visage, and that all the walls<br>With painted imagery had said at once,<br>‘Jesu preserve thee! Welcome, Bolingbroke!’<br>Whilst he, from the one side to the other turning,<br>Bare-headed, lower than his proud steed’s neck,<br>Bespake them thus: ‘I thank you, countrymen’,<br>And thus still doing, thus he passed along. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Alack, poor [[Richard]]! Where rode he the whilst? |
| YORK | As in a theatre the eyes of men,<br>After a [[Well\|well]]-graced actor [[Leaves\|leaves]] the stage,<br>Are idly bent on him that enters next,<br>Thinking his prattle to be tedious,<br>Even so, or with much more contempt, men’s eyes<br>Did scowl on gentle [[Richard]]. No man cried ‘God save him!’<br>No joyful tongue gave him his welcome [[Home\|home]];<br>But [[Dust\|dust]] was thrown upon his sacred head,<br>Which with such gentle sorrow he shook off,<br>His face still combating with [[Tears\|tears]] and smiles,<br>The badges of his grief and patience,<br>That had not God for some strong purpose steeled<br>The hearts of men, they must perforce have melted,<br>And barbarism itself have pitied him.<br>But heaven hath a hand in these events,<br>To whose high will we bound our calm contents.<br>To Bolingbroke are we sworn subjects now,<br>Whose state and [[Honour\|honour]] I for aye allow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Duke of Aumerle]] |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Here comes my son Aumerle. |
| YORK | Aumerle that was;<br>But that is lost for being [[Richard]]’s friend,<br>And, madam, you must call him ’Rutland’ now.<br>I am in Parliament pledge for his [[Truth\|truth]]<br>And lasting fealty to the new-made [[King]]. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Welcome, my son. Who are the violets now<br>That strew the green lap of the new-come [[Spring\|spring]]? |
| AUMERLE | Madam, I know not, nor I greatly care not.<br>God knows I had as lief be none as one. |
| YORK | [[Well]], bear you [[Well\|well]] in this new [[Spring\|spring]] of [[Time\|time]],<br>Lest you be cropped before you come to prime.<br>What news from [[Oxford]]? Hold these jousts and triumphs? |
| AUMERLE | For aught I know, my lord, they do. |
| YORK | You will be there, I know. |
| AUMERLE | If God prevent it not, I purpose so. |
| YORK | What seal is that that hangs without thy bosom?<br>Yea, look’st thou pale? Let me see the writing. |
| AUMERLE | My lord, ’tis nothing. |
| YORK | No matter, then, who see it.<br>I will be satisfied. Let me see the writing. |
| AUMERLE | I do beseech your grace to pardon me.<br>It is a matter of small consequence,<br>Which for some reasons I would not have seen. |
| YORK | Which for some reasons, sir, I mean to see.<br>I [[Fear\|fear]], I [[Fear\|fear]]! |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | What should you [[Fear\|fear]]?<br>‘Tis nothing but some bond that he is entered into<br>For gay apparel ’gainst the triumph day. |
| YORK | Bound to himself? What doth he with a bond<br>That he is bound to? Wife, thou art a [[Fool\|fool]].<br>Boy, let me see the writing. |
| AUMERLE | I do beseech you, pardon me. I may not show it. |
| YORK | I will be satisfied. Let me see it, I say.<br>He plucks it out of Aumerle’s bosom, and reads it<br>Treason, foul treason ! [[Villain]], traitor, slave ! |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | What is the matter, my lord? |
| YORK | Ho, who is within there? Saddle my [[Horse\|horse]].—<br>God for his [[Mercy\|mercy]], what treachery is here I |
| DUCHESS | of YORK Why, what is it, my lord? |
| YORK | Give me my boots, I say. Saddle my [[Horse\|horse]].—<br>Now by mine [[Honour\|honour]], by my life, my troth,<br>I will appeach the [[Villain\|villain]]. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | What is the matter? |
| YORK | Peace, foolish woman. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | I will not peace. What is the matter, son? |
| AUMERLE | Good mother, be content. It is no more<br>Than my poor life must answer. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Thy life answer? |
| YORK | Bring me my boots. I will unto the [[King]].<br>His man enters with his boots |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Strike him, Aumerle! Poor boy, thou art amazed.<br>(To York’s man) Hence, [[Villain\|villain]]! Never more come in my sight. |
| YORK | Give me my boots, I say. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Why, York, what wilt thou do?<br>Wilt thou not hide the trespass of thine own?<br>Have we more sons? Or are we like to have?<br>Is not my teeming date drunk up with [[Time\|time]]?<br>And wilt thou pluck my fair son from mine age,<br>And rob me of a happy mother’s name?<br>Is he not like thee? Is he not thine own? |
| YORK | Thou fond, mad woman,<br>Wilt thou conceal this dark conspiracy?<br>A dozen of them here have ta’en the sacrament,<br>And interchangeably set down their hands<br>To kill the [[King]] at [[Oxford]]. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | He shall be none.<br>We’ll keep him here, then what is that to him? |
| YORK | Away, fond woman! Were he twenty times my son<br>I would appeach him. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Hadst thou groaned for him<br>As I have done thou wouldst be more pitiful.<br>But now I know thy mind: thou dost suspect<br>That I have been disloyal to thy bed,<br>And that he is a bastard, not thy son.<br>Sweet York, sweet husband, be not of that mind.<br>He is as like thee as a man may be,<br>Not like to me or any of my kin,<br>And yet I [[Love\|love]] him. |
| YORK | Make way, unruly woman. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with his man |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | After, Aumerle! Mount thee upon his [[Horse\|horse]].<br>Spur, post, and get before him to the [[King]],<br>And beg thy pardon ere he do accuse thee.<br>I’ll not be long behind—though I be old,<br>I [[Doubt\|doubt]] not but to ride as fast as York—<br>And never will I rise up from the ground<br>Till Bolingbroke have pardoned thee. Away, be gone I |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 5, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bolingbroke, crowned [[King]] Henry, with Harry Percy, and other nobles |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Can no man tell of my unthrifty son?<br>‘Tis full three months since I did see him last.<br>If any plague hang over us, ’tis he.<br>I would to God, my lords, he might be found.<br>Enquire at [[London]] ’mongst the taverns there,<br>For there, they say, he daily doth frequent<br>With unrestrained loose companions—<br>Even such, they say, as stand in narrow lanes<br>And beat our watch and rob our passengers—<br>Which he, young wanton and effeminate boy,<br>Takes on the point of [[Honour\|honour]] to support<br>So dissolute a crew. |
| HARRY PERCY | My lord, some two days since, I saw the Prince,<br>And told him of these triumphs held at [[Oxford]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | And what said the gallant? |
| HARRY PERCY | His answer was he would unto the stews,<br>And from the common’st creature pluck a glove,<br>And wear it as a favour, and with that<br>He would unhorse the lustiest challenger. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | As dissolute as desperate. Yet through both<br>I see some sparks of better [[Hope\|hope]], which elder days<br>May happily bring forth. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Duke of Aumerle]], amazed |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | But who comes here? |
| AUMERLE | Where is the [[King]]? |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | What means our cousin that he stares and looks so wildly? |
| AUMERLE | (kneeling)<br>God save your grace! I do beseech your majesty<br>To have some conference with your grace [[Alone\|alone]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | (to lords)<br>Withdraw yourselves, and leave us here [[Alone\|alone]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[King]] Henry and Aumerle |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | What is the matter with our cousin now? |
| AUMERLE | For ever may my knees grow to the earth,<br>My tongue cleave to the roof within my mouth,<br>Unless a pardon ere I rise or speak. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Intended or committed was this fault?<br>If on the first, how heinous e’er it be,<br>To win thy after-[[Love\|love]] I pardon thee. |
| AUMERLE | (rising)<br>Then give me leave that I may turn the key,<br>That no man enter till my tale be done. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Have thy [[Desire\|desire]].<br>Aumerle locks the door.<br>The [[Duke of York]] knocks at the door and crieth |
| YORK | (within) My liege, beware! Look to thyself!<br>Thou hast a traitor in thy presence there.<br>[[King]] Henry draws his [[Sword\|sword]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | (to Aumerle) [[Villain]], I’ll make thee safe. |
| AUMERLE | Stay thy revengeful hand! Thou hast no cause to [[Fear\|fear]]. |
| YORK | (knocking within)<br>Open the door, secure foolhardy [[King]]!<br>Shall I for [[Love\|love]] speak treason to thy face?<br>Open the door, or I will break it open. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[King]] Henry opens the door. Enter the [[Duke of York]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | What is the matter, uncle? Speak,<br>Recover breath, tell us how near is danger,<br>That we may arm us to encounter it. |
| YORK | Peruse this writing here, and thou shalt know<br>The treason that my haste forbids me show.<br>He gives [[King]] Henry the paper |
| AUMERLE | Remember, as thou read’st, thy promise past.<br>I do repent me. Read not my name there.<br>My heart is not confederate with my hand. |
| YORK | It was, [[Villain\|villain]], ere thy hand did set it down.<br>I tore it from the traitor’s bosom, [[King]].<br>[[Fear]], and not [[Love\|love]], begets his penitence.<br>Forget to pity him, lest pity prove<br>A serpent that will sting thee to the heart. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | O, heinous, strong, and bold conspiracy!<br>O loyal father of a treacherous son!<br>Thou sheer, immaculate, and silver fountain,<br>From whence this stream through muddy passages<br>Hath held his current and defiled himself,<br>Thy overflow of good converts to bad,<br>And thy abundant goodness shall excuse<br>This deadly blot in thy digressing son. |
| YORK | So shall my virtue be his vice’s bawd,<br>And he shall spend mine [[Honour\|honour]] with his shame,<br>As thriftless sons their scraping fathers’ gold.<br>Mine [[Honour\|honour]] lives when his dishonour dies,<br>Or my shamed life in his dishonour lies.<br>Thou kill’st me in his life: giving him breath<br>The traitor lives, the true man’s put to [[Death\|death]]. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | (within)<br>What ho, my liege, for God’s sake let me in! |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | What shrill-voiced suppliant makes this eager cry? |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | (within)<br>A woman, and thy aunt, great [[King]]; ’tis I.<br>Speak with me, pity me! Open the door!<br>A beggar begs that never begged before. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Our scene is altered from a serious thing,<br>And now changed to ‘The Beggar and the [[King]]’.<br>My dangerous cousin, let your mother in.<br>I know she is come to pray for your foul sin.<br>Aumerle opens the door. Enter the Duchess of York |
| YORK | If thou do pardon, whosoever pray,<br>More sins for this [[Forgiveness\|forgiveness]] prosper may.<br>This festered joint cut off, the rest rest sound.<br>This let [[Alone\|alone]] will all the rest confound. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | (kneeling)<br>O [[King]], believe not this hard-hearted man.<br>[[Love]] loving not itself, none other can. |
| YORK | Thou frantic woman, what dost thou make here?<br>Shall thy old dugs once more a traitor rear? |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Sweet York, be patient.—Hear me, gentle liege. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Rise up, good aunt. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Not yet, I thee beseech.<br>Forever will I kneel upon my knees,<br>And never see day that the happy sees,<br>Till thou give joy, until thou bid me joy<br>By pardoning Rutland, my transgressing boy. |
| AUMERLE | (kneeling)<br>Unto my mother’s prayers I bend my knee. |
| YORK | (kneeling)<br>Against them both my true joints bended be.<br>Ill mayst thou thrive if thou grant any grace. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Pleads he in earnest? Look upon his face.<br>His eyes do drop no [[Tears\|tears]], his prayers are in jest.<br>His words come from his mouth; ours from our<br>breast.<br>He prays but faintly, and would be denied;<br>We pray with heart and [[Soul\|soul]], and all beside.<br>His weary joints would gladly rise, I know;<br>Our knees shall kneel till to the ground they grow.<br>His prayers are full of false hypocrisy;<br>Ours of true zeal and deep integrity.<br>Our prayers do outpray his; then let them have<br>That [[Mercy\|mercy]] which true [[prayer]] ought to have. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[King\|KING]] HENRY |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Good aunt, stand up. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Nay, do not say ‘Stand up’.<br>Say ‘Pardon’ first, and afterwards ‘Stand up’.<br>An if I were thy [[Nurse\|nurse]], thy tongue to teach,<br>‘Pardon’ should be the first word of thy speech.<br>I never longed to hear a word till now.<br>Say ‘Pardon’, [[King]]. Let pity teach thee how.<br>The word is short, but not so short as sweet;<br>No word like ’Pardon’ for kings’ mouths so meet. |
| YORK | Speak it in French, [[King]]: say ‘Pardonnez-moi’. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Dost thou teach pardon pardon to destroy?<br>Ah, my sour husband, my hard-hearted lord<br>That sets the word itself against the word!<br>Speak ‘Pardon’ as ’tis current in our land;<br>The chopping French we do not understand.<br>Thine eye begins to speak; set thy tongue there;<br>Or in thy piteous heart plant thou thine ear,<br>That hearing how our plaints and prayers do pierce,<br>Pity may move thee ’Pardon’ to rehearse. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Good aunt, stand up. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | I do not sue to stand.<br>Pardon is all the suit I have in hand. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | I pardon him as God shall pardon me. |
| *(didascalia)* | York and Aumerle rise |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | O, happy vantage of a kneeling knee!<br>Yet am I sick for [[Fear\|fear]]. Speak it again.<br>Twice saying pardon doth not pardon twain,<br>But makes one pardon strong. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | I pardon him<br>With all my heart. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | (rising) A god on earth thou art. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | But for our trusty brother-in-law and the Abbot,<br>With all the rest of that consorted crew,<br>Destruction straight shall dog them at the heels.<br>Good uncle, help to order several powers<br>To [[Oxford]], or where’er these traitors are.<br>They shall not live within this world, I swear,<br>But I will have them if I once know where.<br>Uncle, farewell; and cousin, so adieu.<br>Your mother [[Well\|well]] hath prayed; and prove you true. |
| DUCHESS OF YORK | Come, my old son. I pray God make thee new. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[King]] Henry at one door; York, the Duchess of York, and Aumerle at another door |

### Atto 5, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir Piers Exton, and his Men |
| EXTON | Didst thou not mark the [[King]], what words he spake?<br>‘Have I no friend will rid me of this living [[Fear\|fear]]?’<br>Was it not so? |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST MAN Those were his very words. |
| EXTON | ‘Have I no friend?’ quoth he. He spake it twice,<br>And urged it twice together, did he not? |
| *(didascalia)* | SECOND MAN He did. |
| EXTON | And speaking it, he wishtly looked on me,<br>As who should say ‘I would thou wert the man<br>That would [[divorce]] this terror from my heart’,<br>Meaning the [[King]] at Pomfret. Come, let’s go.<br>I am the [[King]]’s friend, and will rid his foe. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Richard]], [[Alone\|alone]] |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | I have been studying how I may compare<br>This [[Prison\|prison]] where I live unto the world;<br>And for because the world is populous,<br>And here is not a creature but myself,<br>I cannot do it. Yet I’ll hammer it out.<br>My brain I’ll prove the female to my [[Soul\|soul]],<br>My [[Soul\|soul]] the father, and these two beget<br>A generation of still-breeding thoughts;<br>And these same thoughts people this little world<br>In humours like the people of this world.<br>For no thought is contented. The better sort,<br>As thoughts of things divine, are intermixed<br>With scruples, and do set the [[Faith\|faith]] itself<br>Against the [[Faith\|faith]], as thus: ‘Come, little ones’,<br>And then again,<br>‘It is as hard to come as for a camel<br>To thread the postern of a small needle’s eye.’<br>Thoughts tending to [[Ambition\|ambition]], they do plot<br>Unlikely wonders: how these vain weak nails<br>May tear a passage through the flinty ribs<br>Of this hard world, my ragged [[Prison\|prison]] walls;<br>And for they cannot, die in their own [[Pride\|pride]].<br>Thoughts tending to content flatter themselves<br>That they are not the first of fortune’s slaves,<br>Nor shall not be the last—like seely beggars,<br>Who, sitting in the stocks, refuge their shame<br>That many have, and others must, set there;<br>And in this thought they find a kind of ease,<br>Bearing their own misfortunes on the back<br>Of such as have before endured the like.<br>Thus play I in one person many people,<br>And none contented. Sometimes am I [[King\|king]];<br>Then treason makes me wish myself a beggar,<br>And so I am. Then crushing penury<br>Persuades me I was better when a [[King\|king]].<br>Then am I kinged again, and by and by<br>Think that I am unkinged by Bolingbroke,<br>And straight am nothing. But whate’er I be,<br>Nor I, nor any man that but man is,<br>With nothing shall be pleased till he be eased<br>With being nothing.<br>The music plays |
| *(didascalia)* | Music do I hear. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Ha, ha; keep [[Time\|time]]! How sour sweet music is<br>When [[Time\|time]] is broke and no proportion kept.<br>So is it in the music of men’s lives.<br>And here have I the daintiness of ear<br>To check [[Time\|time]] broke in a disordered string;<br>But for the concord of my state and [[Time\|time]]<br>Had not an ear to hear my true [[Time\|time]] broke.<br>I wasted [[Time\|time]], and now doth [[Time\|time]] waste me,<br>For now hath [[Time\|time]] made me his numb‘ring [[Clock\|clock]].<br>My thoughts are minutes, and with sighs they jar<br>Their watches on unto mine eyes, the outward watch<br>Whereto my finger, like a dial’s point,<br>Is pointing still in cleansing them from [[Tears\|tears]].<br>Now, sir, the sounds that tell what hour it is<br>Are clamorous groans that strike upon my heart,<br>Which is the bell. So sighs, and [[Tears\|tears]], and groans<br>Show minutes, hours, and times. But my [[Time\|time]]<br>Runs posting on in Bolingbroke’s proud joy,<br>While I stand fooling here, his jack of the [[Clock\|clock]].<br>This music mads me. Let it sound no more,<br>For though it have holp madmen to their wits,<br>In me it seems it will make wise men mad. |
| *(didascalia)* | The music ceases |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Yet blessing on his heart that gives it me,<br>For ’tis a sign of [[Love\|love]], and [[Love\|love]] to [[Richard]]<br>Is a strange brooch in this all-hating world. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | a Groom<br>of<br>the<br>stable |
| GROOM | Hail, royal Prince! |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Thanks, noble peer.<br>The cheapest of us is ten groats too dear.<br>What art thou, and how com’st thou hither,<br>Where no man never comes but that sad dog<br>That brings me food to make misfortune live? |
| GROOM | I was a poor groom of thy stable, [[King]],<br>When thou wert [[King\|king]]; who, travelling towards York,<br>With much ado at length have gotten leave<br>To look upon my sometimes royal master’s face.<br>O, how it erned my heart when I beheld<br>In [[London]] streets, that coronation day,<br>When Bolingbroke rode on roan Barbary,<br>That [[Horse\|horse]] that thou so often hast bestrid,<br>That [[Horse\|horse]] that I so carefully have dressed! |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Rode he on Barbary? Tell me, gentle friend,<br>How went he under him? |
| GROOM | So proudly as if he disdained the ground. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | So proud that Bolingbroke was on his back.<br>That jade hath eat bread from my royal hand;<br>This hand hath made him proud with clapping him.<br>Would he not stumble, would he not fall down-<br>Since [[Pride\|pride]] must have a fall—and break the neck<br>Of that proud man that did usurp his back?<br>[[Forgiveness]], [[Horse\|horse]]! Why do I rail on thee,<br>Since thou, created to be awed by man,<br>Wast born to bear? I was not made a [[Horse\|horse]],<br>And yet I bear a burden like an ass,<br>Spur-galled and tired by jauncing Bolingbroke. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Keeper to [[Richard]], with meat |
| KEEPER | (to Groom)<br>Fellow, give place. Here is no longer stay. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | (to Groom)<br>If thou [[Love\|love]] me, ’tis [[Time\|time]] thou wert away. |
| GROOM | What my tongue dares not, that my heart shall say. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| KEEPER | My lord, will’t please you to fall to? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | Taste of it first, as thou art wont to do. |
| KEEPER | My lord, I dare not. Sir Piers of Exton,<br>Who lately came from the [[King]], commands the contrary. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | (striking<br>the Keeper)<br>The devil take Henry of Lancaster and thee I<br>Patience is stale, and I am weary of it. |
| KEEPER | Help, help, help!<br>Exton and his men rush in |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | How now! What means [[Death\|death]] in this rude assault?<br>He seizes a weapon from a man, and kills him<br>[[Villain]], thy own hand yields thy [[Death\|death]]’s instrument.<br>He kills another<br>Go thou, and fill another room in hell.<br>Here Exton strikes him down |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] | That hand shall burn in never-quenching [[Fire\|fire]]<br>That staggers thus my person. Exton, thy fierce hand<br>Hath with the [[King]]’s [[Blood\|blood]] stained the [[King]]’s own land.<br>Mount, mount, my [[Soul\|soul]]; thy seat is up on high,<br>Whilst my gross flesh sinks downward, here to die.<br>He dies |
| EXTON | As full of valour as of royal [[Blood\|blood]].<br>Both have I spilt. O, would the deed were good I<br>For now the devil that told me I did [[Well\|well]]<br>Says that this deed is chronicled in hell.<br>This dead [[King]] to the living [[King]] I’ll bear.<br>Take hence the rest, and give them burial here. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Exton with [[Richard]]’s [[Body\|body]] at one door, and his men with the other bodies at another door |
| *(didascalia)* | 5.6 Flourish. Enter [[King]] Henry and the [[Duke of York]], with other lords and attendants |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Kind uncle York, the latest news we hear<br>Is that the rebels have consumed with [[Fire\|fire]]<br>Our town of Ci’cester in Gloucestershire;<br>But whether they be ta’en or slain we hear not. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Northumberland]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Welcome, my lord. What is the news? |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | First, to thy sacred state wish I all happiness.<br>The next news is, I have to [[London]] sent<br>The heads of Salisbury, Spencer, Blunt, and [[Kent]].<br>The manner of their taking may appear<br>At large discoursed in this paper here.<br>He gives the paper to [[King]] Henry |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | We thank thee, gentle Percy, for thy pains,<br>And to thy worth will add right worthy gains. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lord Fitzwalter |
| FITZWALTER | My lord, I have from [[Oxford]] sent to [[London]]<br>The heads of Brocas and Sir Bennet Seely,<br>Two of the dangerous consorted traitors<br>That sought at [[Oxford]] thy dire overthrow. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Thy pains, Fitzwalter, shall not be forgot.<br>Right noble is thy merit, [[Well\|well]] I wot. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Harry Percy, with the Bishop of Carlisle, guarded |
| HARRY PERCY | The grand conspirator Abbot of Westminster,<br>With clog of [[Conscience\|conscience]] and sour [[Melancholy\|melancholy]],<br>Hath yielded up his [[Body\|body]] to the [[Grave\|grave]].<br>But here is Carlisle living, to abide<br>Thy kingly doom and sentence of his [[Pride\|pride]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Carlisle, this is your doom.<br>Choose out some secret place, some reverent room<br>More than thou hast, and with it joy thy life.<br>So as thou liv’st in peace, die free from strife.<br>For though mine enemy thou hast ever been,<br>High sparks of [[Honour\|honour]] in thee have I seen. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Exton with his men bearing the coffin |
| EXTON | Great [[King]], within this coffin I present<br>Thy buried [[Fear\|fear]]. Herein all breathless lies<br>The mightiest of thy greatest enemies,<br>[[Richard]] of Bordeaux, by me hither brought. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Exton, I thank thee not, for thou hast wrought<br>A deed of slander with thy fatal hand<br>Upon my head and all this famous land. |
| EXTON | From your own mouth, my lord, did I this deed. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | They [[Love\|love]] not [[Poison\|poison]] that do [[Poison\|poison]] need;<br>Nor do I thee. Though I did wish him dead,<br>I hate the murderer, [[Love\|love]] him murdered.<br>The [[Guilt\|guilt]] of [[Conscience\|conscience]] take thou for thy labour,<br>But neither my good word nor princely favour.<br>With [[Cain]] go wander through the shades of [[Night\|night]],<br>And never show thy head by day nor light. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Exton and his men |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Lords, I protest my [[Soul\|soul]] is full of woe<br>That [[Blood\|blood]] should sprinkle me to make me grow.<br>Come mourn with me for what I do lament,<br>And put on sullen black incontinent.<br>I’ll make a voyage to the Holy Land<br>To wash this [[Blood\|blood]] off from my guilty hand.<br>March sadly after. Grace my mournings here<br>In weeping after this untimely bier. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt With the coffin |
| ADDITIONAL PASSAGES | The following passages of four lines or more appear in the 1597 Quarto but not the Folio; Shakespeare probably deleted them as part of his limited revisions to the text.<br>a. . AFTER 1.3.127<br>And for we think the eagle-winged [[Pride\|pride]]<br>Of sky-aspiring and ambitious thoughts<br>With rival-hating envy set on you<br>To wake our peace, which in our country’s cradle<br>Draws the sweet infant breath of gentle [[Sleep\|sleep]],<br>b.
